LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAR NELL/B, The seaside requires a nautical dress to corres- pond with the surroundings, and all kinds of taste- ful designs are being made up for the purpose. The best costume is that of taut and trim aspect and the general appropriateness of her attire to the nautical occasion. Serge is, by virtue of its pre- eminent suitability to the circumstances, the material most in vogue. This year a combination of serge and taffetas .is much to the fore, and is employed as bands, trimmings, revecs, and waist- coats. Fashion never gets far from the sailor collar, shirt, and tie, and anyone wearing thesc will not be far wroug. Avoid useless fripperies and materials that spoil with contact with sea air and water.. A train, I may obnpve, on a yachting dress is an anomaly; let the 'shirt touch the deck all round, but hwe nothing that serves the double purpDse of a deck mop and a snare for the unwary to trip up in. Yachting skirts should Ise suffici- ently heavy to hang solidly. If you follow cut these directions you can rely upou a sensible gar- ment.

'ompons, thoge fluffy white haile are ma!zing

~eir way to the fr..‘tit, and sery t vary useful de for summer wear. -frats of silk, muslin, and n hav, ben a good deal in evidence lately. They are p'ettiresqua and light wear, hut inclinca to tiiik that' they Ina only tiito.Lio for youLhful wearers. They should certainly never be worn with a veil, whicli spoils the effect of all fancy or artistic hats. They Must thua be limited to oc.mmions, as veils so desirable for practical wear to keep the cotrfare in order, for nothing looks worse, oz: such bad form, now, as an unkempt dishevelled head of hair.

I am rather inc:incil to think that coats and skirts are still the favourite wear foc the seaside. They have now boon with iv, now for some time and I think they will stay evun some time longer. They are made in all shapes, but I fancy the square-cut ones. A good stock of shirts is, there- fore, necessary, and nothing is so nice as a soft silk; it does not crush, and when the coat is removed the shirt is guiltless of creasings. Fine flannel is an excellent material, and can now be had in the most delicate colourings and designs. If you prefer a cotton shirt select one of good batiste, as it is less likely to be crushed than zephyr, or piqué.

I saw yesterday such a nice gown of pale dove. grey cashmere, with trimmings of cream guipure lace insertions, worn over an under-bodice and sleeves of white sills, horizontally piped from top to bottom. The bodice, both under and over, was slightly pouched. The skirt had two rows of the cream guipure running down each of the two front seams of tne skirt as now cut, and was fastened by an opening at the top of the left-hand one, con- cealed by the lace, thus leavin; the quite plain back—guiltless of placket-hole—de-,ired now by the ultra-fashionable.

If you are thinking of bathing at the seaside I must remind you that the fashionable variety fastens down the back, and you must have someone to put it on and take it off. Iliaok silk stockings and white shoes are worn with smart bathing costumes. Orange and grey form the colouring of the calas devised for decided brunettozi and bright green or a turquoise blue, blended with a pale sepia tint, characteirses tae 9aps intended for blondes. SO'll,J very caciaevtteirsll firmly ladies tohavteneiar " bithing triage" attaehel y caps. o:nora nave the hied ou'Aineseoucealed by means of a coioure I handkerchief twisted, t'..rban- fashion, round the edge, and tied in two "dogs' ears" that stand erect above the forehead. Many of the bathing dresses are of serge chiefly white. Aoout tFii p trt of the year is the time to turn out wardrobes and chests of drawers. The plague of moths which Feerns to have made havoc with one's clothes this seaion demands that we should indulge in this pi.stim) now and again. Some of our gar wants can be ranovated and made fashion- able. I have a tailor•in Ile blue :serge suit. The coat is of reefer shape with square revers of velvet braided at the edges. It is stiil a neat, stylish little garment, but the skirt is dreadful—far too short :or the present and altogether the wrong shape. It is a wide skirt, and I shall have it re- modelled. The rkirt hal been let down and the cut place covered with braid. This I offer to you as an example for all serge or cloth suits which want remodelling.

Your loving JENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Since the Queen studied the fashions many years te.avo pivsed. So long as new garments are made precisely and exactly on the lines of old ones, Her Majesty never troubles herself about clothes. In bonnets only has she allowed the slieht-st deviation from the beaten track, and this state of affairs is mainly owing to the Princess of Wales. She it was who persuaded Her Majesty to allow the graceful tuft of white feathers to appear in her headgear, and the various other touches of white in her toilette, which prove SD becoming to the Sovereign. Th" Princess of Wales, and the other hand, is most particular that her clothes shall be irreproachable both in cut and style. The fit of a gown must be absolut-ly perfect, and no alteration in a bodice is ever permitted. That Her Royal Highness is difficult to please in this particular matter is an undeniable fact. Few things rale the Princess's generally placid temper but an ill- fitting grown will accomplish this underairable end in an amazingly short space of time. In an apartment set aside for the purpose, at Sand- ringham, the Princess of Wales has had preserved a specimen of each variety of headgear indulged in by Her Royal Highness since her arrival in this country. The collection however, is not so extensive as cne might imagine. For years the Princess patronised the small, neat bonnet which bears her name, and for some time past now, the little tcque has reigned supreme in her affections.

Among Society women, Lady Algernon Gordon-Len- nox stands an asy first, in the matter of clothes an how to wear them. She is never seen in an unbecoming w.vvti, and she is always irreproachably " fini,hed off." Lady Foe Sturt has also a good reputat'on for good dressing, and Madame Van Andre—the beautiful American—is also an exquisite dresser. Few not in the swim, can realise what it means to be a leader of fashion. Unlimited funds—or ore.ait, and as much anxie,y acf the Government of an Empire involves. For one unbecoming toilette or ill-arranged head of hair, is almost enough to ruin the reputation of a Scciety leader.

There is no such thing as living within one's income. The prevailing method secms to ea to overstep the mark more or less considerably, and when the cresti comes, as it does to so many nowadays, the hollowness of society at length makes itself apparent to the victims. Friends are not gained by display and extravagant living. Such a life only surrounds us with social butterflies ho vanish at the faintest sign of storm. In olded days, many of the titles to books, partook more of the nature of an advertisement or prospectus than a title proper, but we have changed all that, and, short catchy titles are the great aim among writers now-a-days. They realise and rightly that an unattrac- tive name may kill the chances of a really good work and further that a good selling title may do wonders for an inferior publication. That the present day titles however, are, many of them, misleading, is a fact well known to all. Vi ho has not, at one time or another, ordered or chosen a book under the impression that it dealt with the subject its title indicated ? In a Londen library the other day, I witnessed a curious little sceee of this description. A girl, bluestocking unmistakable, walked up to an assistant and asked for "Moths." Tbe book was handed to her,when she opened it eagerly, ex- pecting of course, to find profuse illustrationseand much learned information. The air of disgust with which she returned it when she rea:ised that "Moths" was a novel only, was comical in the extreme, but I could but sym- pathise w it'a her in her disappointment. A good title should besides being attractive, be descriptive of the work it labels, although some allusiveness or double meaning may add to its charm. Fashions just now are at a standstill. Nothing new is to be seen, and mediates are resting for a brief span. Little has transpired so far, as to autumn fashions, but we may rest assured that plain skirts of more generous proportions about the hips, will prevail, and Ghat coats of the newest and closest build, will be the favourite coveting. Frills, flounces, tunics etc., are fast dis- appearing, but skirts are still worn as long for walking, as for the house. Those of us who value our health will do well to set our faces against the dirty, untidy, and unwholesome mode. _ _ _

The present fashion of twining the hair round the Wick of the bead rather fiat, and pinned well in to define the outline of the head, is, generally speaking, a becoming one, and to the girl who cycles it is a boon, for it per- mits of an elastic to held on her hat, which good old- fashioned manner of fixing, all the patent arrangements or hat pins in Christendom have not been able to beat. some of our leading milliners are furnishing their cycling headgear with brown and fawn elastic, and endeavour to match toe colour of their customers hair, as far as possible. " MADGE."

THE NATIONAL BURNS MEMORIAL AT

MAUCHLINE.

Readers of " Quilp N's" article in our issue of last week entitled" A Visit to the Land o' Cakes" will probably recollect that reference was made to the memodul which has been erected at lifauchline in hcnour of the genius of Burnes. The treasurer has this week forwarded us the following outburst in verse : Sir,— A last appeal we mak' tae ye ; Gie this a place whare a' may see How still we're short by hundreds three 0' thousands five, The sum we thccht wad needed be Mak' oor scheme thrive. It's owre four rare sin' we began To set oor brains weep an' plan Enthusiasm's Mine how best to fan Far an' pear baith, When time a hundred years wad span Frae Robin's death.

WQ mind't h.w thcre on Mauehline Green Burns first beh Id his Bonnie Jean, An' at losgiel wi' gentle mien

Dd Coila's.-Muse Crown him the Bard o'Scotia, e'en

Her art infuse. Statues o'the Bard the wand o'er There are we're sure at !east a score ; Nae need we thocht there waa fr,r more, Sae plann'd a tower

For relics, homes fcr auld folks poor, An' begged for power. At first we had a fairish start, By tryin' every wile and art Our brain could frame, to reach ilk heart An' draw out cash; But stale our trials came fee smart,

Wi' muekle fah. The "Indian Famine Fund " cam' round To gie to which a' folk were bound; An' Abdul-Bainid, d--d vile hound, Armenians slew, For help bis victims had good ground,

An' gat it too. - Our wild Queen's Diamond Jubilee Next brocht a scheme for i!lc bawbee That could be spared, an' bond or free

Had each their need—

Infirmaries, lifeboats, land and sea, Some special plead. Now they're a' past, an' we hae here Guid crops, guid trade, an' little fear 0' strikes, " lockouts," this present year ; Then gle's a hand. An' help us weel our feet to clear, Is our demand.

An' ye wha help for Robin's sake, Gar tottering age mair comfort take, Gie poortith cauld an unco shake, Gainsay't ye' fear , Where Rob's ye'll muckle pleasure make, Cause joyfu' tear. Thomas Kirmur, Hon. Treasurer. 168, West George-street, Glasgow.

THE COUNTRY CHURCI.

When smiles the morn that Gad hath blest With light and joy serene; How loves the quiet eye to rest On some familiar scene. The little church on yonder hill More sacred then appears; A refuge for the weary still, Amidst life's toils and tears. Our fathers, by devotion moved, For God that temple made : And in iti courts, se much beloved, Their solemn vows were paid. But now beneath its shadow lone Their graves lie side by side : Some with, and some without, a stone To tell us when they died. The hand cf time all gently falls On that dear house of prayer: The ivy still festeons its walls, And sparrows.chirrup there. And still as holy days come round, Fresh blessings to unfold; The lowly in its pews are found, As in the days of old. There poor and rich in common meet, Their end and hopes the same ; And gath'ring round the Mercy seat, They feel devotion's flame. One spirit fills the pious throng, And moulds their simple prayers : One joy pervades their swelling song, One hope of glory theirs. Thus in their fathers' steps they tread And feel the bliss they knew : Still en-gard by the Spirit led, " To bhate their glory too. Long may that hallow'd fane display The light that points on high To guide men in the narrow way, And cheer them when they die. Warrington. J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 18, 1899

(Copyright.) THE

REVENGE OF VALERIE:

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA.

BY HUME NISBET,

Author of "A Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," "Hunting for Gold, "The Swampers," &c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Sir Lewis Eastlake, twenty-four years of age, is intro- duced to the reader, as he is "going the pace," at a London gambling club. There, in company with a friend, Captain Eric St. Arnoul, he gets into an altercation, which results in a duel, at Cherbourg. His opponent is killed in this. He has a strong sentimental friendship with the Duchess of Valerie, at Genoa, a widow lady, in the prime of her beauty, who, in affection for him, backs the financier, who lends him money. Captain St. Arnoul, his com- panion, who is a cousin of the Duchess, informs her that Sir Lewis had been injured in the duel, and, in view of the other man's death, it is thought prudent he should be removed from Cherbourg. The Duchess hears, that he is in charge of a Cherbourg doctor, who is an old friend, and his sister, who is a nurse. She fears that he may be arrested, and arranges with the Captain for him to be taken by the nurse to her seat at Falaise, and she follows on afterwards. The Duchess finds, to her regret. on her arrival at Falaise, that the nurse "Hope" is young and good-looking, and a friend of Sir Lewis irom childhood. After Hope has left he tells the Duchess that he and Hope had been engaged for years, and that before the duel they were married privately. Sir Lewis Eastlake goes out to Klondike, and is followed by a man who calls himself Bill Sykes, and appears to be acting under instructions. Hope Ea,stlake's brother dies in Cherbourg, and she finds herself moneyless and alone. A letter which she writes to her husband never reaches him, but is sent to the Duchess Valerie, who keeps it.

CHAPTER XI.

HOPE LEAVES THE HOUSE OF MRS. SOLOMON DAVIS.

How wearily the months passed in that stuffy and cramped back room! There were only those uni- form bricks and windows to look at from the window of Hope Eastlake, with occasionally a discontented face peering down coldly upon her. She saw the same faces day after day, or possibly now and again a new face after the same cold and colourless pattern. None of the faces ever smiled, as they looked forth from those white-curtained windows. She saw them in the early morning, and at all times of the forenoon, raise their blinds and look out with sullen brows. They were then dis- hevelled, pasty-visaged and sodden women, and dirty-looking, ghastly men. She had no oppor- tunity of seeing how these faces appeared later on in the day, being located at the back regions. Hope often speculated wonderingly in her mind how these men and women managed to exist. They appeared so much alike in their unkempt condi- tion that she thought they must all belong to one trade or profession; also, that the business did not make them happy or sociable. Hope had yet to learn that the expression which she saw on the morning faces of those Brixtonians was the habi- tual expression of London people when they are looking out of windows on their neighbours, and across the space of a 'bus or train. This universal expression freezes the heart of country-bred strangers when they first come to London. They attempt to smile, look cheerful, and even to break the grim spell of speechlessness that hangs about, but no responsive smile answers theirs. A stony glare rewards their bravery and they are crushed. By-and-bye they grow used to this protective hedge of reserve, and become grim- mouthed and wall-eyed, like their neighbours. Then their city education is complete. They take their place in the ranks of voiceless spectres and smile no more. Once the habit is lost it can no more be recovered than can innocence. When these city spectres stretch their lips it is only to grin in a skin-cracking fashion over some stupidity or folly in those about them. When they try to laugh it is a mirthless and artificial cachinnation. They can grin, chuckle, or cackle, but they cannot smile or laugh in large cities. Hope Eastlake felt the utter dreariness and isolation of her position very acutely during the first months of her sojourn in Brixton. In the house there were ever al lodgers; how many she never found out, as she seldom encountered any of them; and when she did, beyond an abrupt "Good morning," or "Good evening," none of them invited further intimacy. The landlady and her husband occupied a double room on the ground floor, and appeared to have a considerable number of visitors of both sexes. These visitors entered and left at all hours of the day and night, gliding in and out in a most dis- creet and silent manner. There were no children in the house. On the second-floor a young couple occupied the front room, while the other two bed- rooms were let to two ladies. Members of the dramatic profession, Mrs. Davis told Hope they were, which accounted for the gorgeous manner in which they dressed when they went forth in the evenings. Hope lived in the upper flat, where the servant also slept, in a little box-room, while the front room was reserved for casual lodgers, strangers who seldom stayed longer than a night at a time. It was a shabby house, both outside and in, and not too cleanly kept. Mr. and Mrs. Solomon Davies might have been lodgers also, for, beyond collecting the rent each Monday, they did not interfere with the other residents. The paint on the doors and skirtings was dingy, dry and worn. The wall-papers also shewed years of wear, and the oilcloths on lobbies and stairs were frayed and a mass of patches. Outside, the railing and gate were rusty, while the paint on windows and front door had been sun-baked almost to powder. Hope always knew her own lodgings without looking at the number, for it was one of the most neglected in the street. -

She made no acquaintances during these months. The shopkeepers who served her treated her with the cold contempt that her small orders seemed to deserve in their estimation. She went to church regularly morning and evening, but no one took any notice of her, and when the curate learnt where she lived he looked very coldly upon her afterwards, nor did he call upon her.

, Acting upon the advice of her supposed friend— Mr. Stephen Belwood—she waited on him to find her employment; yet while she waited she invested a portion of her money in a typewriter and occu- pied herself by learning to use it. He seemed delighted when she shewed him the machine, and informed her that it was a good investment. Mr. Stephen Belwood called upon her pretty frequently during the first few weeks of her sojourn in Brixton. He cheered her up by saying that she must not be anxious at no news coming -of; from America, the distance being so great and postal arrangements not being in force yet in those regions. This comforted her greatly for a time. After, however, the money-lender had secured that cheque his visits became rarer, until they finally ceased. Yet he did not neglect her altogether, for he wrote several times, and sent one of his confidential clerks out to see how she was getting along. Hope felt grateful for these attentions, even if she did not like the messenger. He was a different man from his master, handsomer a good deal, but much too bold and familiar to please Hope. This young man lived in Clapham, and it seemed her fate to meet him often in the streets when she was out walking. On these occasions he presumed to accost her and walk with her, much to her annoyance. Yet she was too gentle and shy by nature to treat him as he deserved, nor did he give her any definite excuse for cutting him.

Hope held up bravely until the summer was far advanced, thinking each week would bring her a letter from her husband. She had mastered the mysteries of the type machine and inserted adver- tisements for work in the papers, but as yet no commissions had come in.

She dared not give way to despair with that trial before her. The self-confident clerk told her not to be in any hurry, that things would be all right. People were mostly out of town during the summer, but when they returned she would be sure le get employment. Once, and once only, Walter Gordon, the confi- dential clerk, was received by Hope Eastlake with hearty welcome. He brought her a half-sheet, which had been enclosed to the money-lender.. There were characteristics about this letter which would have raised instant suspicions in the mind of an expert as to its genuineness. Hope was not an expert, however, and never doubted. It was written with a pencil on a leaf torn from his pocket-book, and conveyed a very brief message that her husband, the writer, was well, but that he had lost his stock of paper and envelopes, and could get none in Dawson City. "The business communication that came to the

governor did not amount to any more than to tell us that on: claims were all right," said the clerk "They were wrapped in newspaper, with the address, as you see, at the foot of each. Our agent at Wrangel forwarded them on in one envelope." Hope was satisfied, and lived on that forged scrap for months afterwards; indeed, it kept. her alive until her baby was born. In the month of October her infant son came to give her a new interest in life. It seemed also to have brought with it the promise of good luck. The doctor who attended her was a sympathetic as well as a sensible and clever man. Before she was better he had gleaned from her what her cir- cumstances were, also as much of her story as she cared to tell any stranger. He proved himself to be a good man, by advising her to quit her present lodgings as soon as she was able to move. Ile did not tell her why she should do this, or, rather, he told her that it was a badly drained street, and promised to look out a more healthy locality for her. He did more than this, for without promising her anything he resolved to give her a trial as a nurse as soon as she was able and he had the opportunity of finding her a good patient. The woman she had to wait upon her during her illness was a creature recommended by Mrs. Solomon Davis—a veritable hag of the Sarah Gamp order, who. in two weeks, wasted what might have kept her for months. So completely did this beldame demoralise the order and. economy of her life and pollute the apartment, that she left Hope with but one desire, which was to clear out as rapidly as she coted. Mrs. Solomon Davis displayed the ugly side to her character when she heard that her lodger intended to leave. She made a vulgar outcry about the extra trouble she had been put to with the doctor and nurse being in the house, and, to the horror of Hope, she made use of some very objectionable language. Next morning, however, after a talk with Mr. Solomon, she altered her hostile attitude and apologised for her rudeness the night before. , "You see, my dear, it appeared like a reflection on my good name, you going off like this, so soon after baby was born. I hope no one has been trying to prejudice you against me, or say that we ain't respectable here ? " Hope eagerly assured her that it was only on the score of health she was going, that the doctor had advised more open space. "Ah, yes. I guessed it was that doctor, but don't you believe him. This is as healthy a house as any in or about Brixton, and a deal more respectable than many." Mrs. Davis made a great point about her re- spectability, which, by the way, Hope had never thought of doubting. Seeing, however, that Hope was firm on the point of moving, she yielded with a fairly good grace. "You won't forsake us altogether, will you ? " she said with a hypocritical, fawning air. "You'll bring down the sweet baby-boy, and have a cup of tea with us of a Sunday now and then, won't you now, my dear? Promise me you will, now, and I'll try to bear the sorrow of parting with you. You've been here so long now that I have grown as fond of you as if you was a sister." Hope, in her eager desire to part friendly, gave the desired promise, and also, so that letters might be forwarded, her new address.

The good doctor was as prompt with his assist- ance as Mr. Stephen Belwood had been dilatory and insincere. He found for her a tidy apartment, to suit her means, in one of the old-fashioned streets of quaint Clapham, not yet demolished by the building fiend. Its days were numbered, how- ever, as the lease had only a year or so to expire. Mrs. Hambly was a hard-working, honest widow, who carried on the business of a laundry in this ancient cottage. She had been brought to it a bride by her husband, who had keen born and died in it. Her pleasures and her sorrows had all been found within that porch-protected, vine-covered front .door. Her children had been courted here and gone out to take the responsibilities of life on their shoulders, with fond recollections of the "old house." \Alien the County Council would remove it, as they were bound to do—for undoubtedly it was greatly in the way of modern advance—they would inflict a sorrow on the Widow Hambly next to the sorrow she had felt when her husband had been removed.

There was a long garden to the back, which con- tributed not a little to the support of Mrs. Hambly. Here she cultivated almost as many vegetables sts she required. Her fowls also added to her income considerably. The doctor knew this humble cottage would please his patient better than a more ambitious modern apartment. The room the Widow Hambly assigned to Hope faced the south-west, and with the old vine clambering over the sides of the window appeared almost rural. The furniture was aged like the vine, and only one of the chairs was absolutely trustworthy; but it felt like a home. Mrs. Hambly was old-fashioned and motherly, not a harpy look- ing out for victims. Dr. Aimhurst was a big, burly man, homely in his ways, up to date in his profession, and possess- ing a heart of gold. • The poor worshipped him and the wealthy patients trusted him utterly. He debated in his mind whether he should give Hope a hint as to the character of the house she had quitted, in time, he trusted, for her fair fame. It was a delicate subject to speak about to a poor and ignorant lady. To tell her that she had been living under the roof of a person as notorious as Mrs. Solomon Davis proved a task too great for his moral courage. A doctor is like a priest in some respects. There are secrets which he must not reveal.

He contented himself, therefore, with telling her that the street she had left was a most unwholesome one, and that she ought not, for the sake of her health, to go near it again. He told her also not to mention where she had been living last to her new landlady or anyone. Hope, all unsuspiciously, promised to act accord- ing to his advice. She did not like concealments of any kind, but she felt that her kind friend Dr. Aimhurst must have the best of reasons for asking her to keep this a secret.

and him a Canadian, so that they tan an active and kindly interest in whomever lives under the protection of the Union Jack. The cold was intense, but the temperature was dry and settled. As they flew over the level parts there was none of the depression felt in a humid atmosphere. Every breath they drew through their respirators made the blood course through their veins as if fired with generous light wine. They had no leisure to feel the cold, for every hour of the daylight was occupied when they crossed the level ground, and there was little enough of that; they had mountains to scale and countless other hardships to face which kept them in a glow- ing condition. The dogs were the most difficult to keep, for they had most voracious appetites, and if not quickly supplied would devour anything that was nearest. The reindeer were, however, easily kept, as every- where their food, the lichen, abounded.

It was apparently a sterile and desolate country over which they went, but to travellers as experi- enced as these were it was not so in reality. They seldom wanted firewood, for if the trees were dwarfish they were plentiful everywhere; easy to cut down, and not difficult to light. Game also was not scarce after they got a hundred miles away from the mining centre. Ptarmigan and white hares were shot almost daily; now and again a wandering drove of musk ox and wild reindeer were sighted and stalked. Then they had excitement as well as hard work to make them sleep sound when they all crept under the tent-flap of a night. Bill Sykes had brought a good supply of stores with him, which, with the exception of some of the dried fruit and preserved vegetables, he seldom had to touch.

He had introduced two novelties to the Klondike miners in the matter of food, which had proved instant favourites—baked pea and lentil flour. Hitherto they had been eating beans and bacon, both good enough in their way to produce warmth and sustain life. But Bill had discovered from a Scotchman how to make a substantial meal in a few moments from a couple of spoonfuls of pea flour. This was in reality the pulse that had nourished the Hebrew children in the palace of the Great King; the sausages that Bismarck fed his legions upon during their winter campaign in France.

A sackful of this flour would provide a man with satisfying food for six months. The longer he used it the better he liked it. It was pre-eminently the food for the Yukon or the Arctic, heat-producing, satisfying, tasty, easily made and easily carried. When Bill Sykes bestowed that boon upon Dawson City it wiped a lot of former delinquencies from his slate.

It was easily made: a handful of the flour put into a pannikin, with a lump of butter or fat, and a little salt. Then some boiling water and a brisk stir-about, and a most succulent paste was the zesult. A paste to put brains into the thinker, muscles into the worker, and true manhood into the debilitated. With this beneficent mixture on hand scurvy could not possibly find a footing. It cleared the complexion, restored the coatings which bad whiskey had burnt from sorely-tried stomachs, renewed youthful vigour, and spread a wave of unusual hope and happiness ovee,this City of Gold. Bill had only one regret, and that was, he could not patent it. As a naturalised American he would have liked to have disguised it and sold it in small packets under some other name, but he had given the secret away before the great importance of it dawned upon him. He had a couple of sacks of this stuff with him now, and, having proved its anti-scorbutic pro- perties, he made up and gave to his men a daily quantity. They all enjoyed the mixture hugely, as they had become used to it before. "With my gun, a good store of ammunition, and half a dozen sacks of pea and lentil flour, I'm prepared to find the North Pole with any man," Bill had said to the Dawsonians, and after they had tested his special mixture they were prepared to believe him.

Bill and his companions had many adventures as they went along, yet proved that there are no im- possible obstacles to prevent a plucky party from crossing the North-West Territory even in the most extreme weather. The Indians did it constantly. Why, then, should not the conquerors and pioneers of civilisation?

Bill Sykes had endured the heat of the gulches' of Colorado. His lungs were sound and his diges- tion perfect. Therefore, he laughed at the fierce ice-breath of the Arctic.

He was a prodigious favourite with the Indians and half-breeds whom he commanded. He was a dead shot with his gun, and absolutely fearless. He was a firm master, yet by no means tyrannical or unreasonable. What he expected them to do he was equally ready to do himself. In many ways Bill Sykes, murderer and outlaw from Texas, was a born commander.

He tackled grizzlies, brown and black bears, on the ledges. Once they encountered a hungry polar white bear, which had prowled down from the Arctic coasts in search of food. They camped with Indians, and were put on the track of those they were searching for. They encountered all the varied fortunes of Argonauts to those climes. Snowstorms came; blizzards broke over tem. They dragged their sledges over deadly slopes and passes —clung to Alpine-like ledges—and escaped death in its many aspects as they struggled over that snow and ice-locked region. At last they came upon the men they were after. It was nearly the close of March before they reached them, and never had relief-party arrived so opportunely. They found the two men in a hut by the banks of a frozen creek at the foot of one of the passe R. They were reduced almost to the last extremity, and had been for the past three weeks existing on starvation rations.

"Another week," said Jack Wade, "and we should have had to wolf our moccasins, while see here! we have fifty thousand dollars in gold-dust already taken out of our claim." Jack Wade was the least affected as yet by the hardships which these two prospectors had ex- perienced. He was a strong man, thickset and broad-shouldered; besides, he had been used to a hard life, which his companion had not. Lewis Eastlake was hors de combat. An unvary- ing diet of salt pork and tinned meat had given him a bad attack of scurvy. He was dropsical, leaden-hued, and almost in the last stages of prostration. Bill Sykes at once constituted himself doctor and cook. He got out his pe.tse flour and condensed milk and sent his half-breeds and Indians on the hunt for fresh meat. In three days the invalid was able to take an interest in mundane affairs; then Bill thought of the letters which he had to deliver. Perhaps taese would put fresh vigour into the frame of the scurvy-devoured man. There was a latter from. the Secretary of the Klondike Moose Mining Company, with a draft for his expenses and salary as director. , There were three long and tender epistles from the Duchess Valerie. In one of these she t id him that his wife had left Cherbourg suddenly, but for where the writer had not been able to discover. In her last letter she informed him that she intended taking a long sea voyage in her yacht, round the Horn and up the Yukon River. She would be at Dawson City about July that year. No letters from his wife. He had not had one since he left her. What could it mean? He had written to her regularly also—that is, du ing the past summer, while lie was at Dawson City. Bill Sykes saw that the letters he had brought did not have the good effect he expected. There was a French paper that he had not yet delivered. Per- chance that might give his Patient more heart. He fished it out from his packets and gave it him. Yes, the French paper roused the gold-finder up. He opened it, saw a place marked with blue pencil, and read the poisoned paragraph with glaring eyes. The French dress their scandal as they dress their dishes—artistically. In this delicately-spiced article Sir Lewis Eastlake learnt his full degrada- tion. His wife had eloped with an Englishman. This paper said rothitig about the death of Dr. Waldred. A former issue had mentioned that disaster. _ _ _ _ . _

•The young gold-finder crushed the cursed paper in his hand and bent his head upon his Ileast. He was too weak Fet to give way to passion; he could only brood upon his disgrace. Hope, the companion of his boyhood, his girl sweetheart, to leave him like this!

A certain sense of retributive justice kept him from judging her too harshly. He was feeling, in his bodily weakness, that he had partly deserved this punishment. He had not thought of marrying her until he was a ruined man. Instead of doing so he had wasted his means, like the prodigal, in riotous living. He had only given her and her brother a burden of debt. "I didn't expect this from Hope," he thought dismally. "Yet I suppose she is like the rest of us. Perhaps I was not as devoted as she expected during those months at Falaise. Perhaps, knowing me as she did, she could not believe I could ever retrieve my evil luck, and so went off with a better man."

CHAPTER XII.

BILL SYKES BRINGS BAD NEWS. '

No man 'could call in question the reckless courage of that bold buccaneer of the Sierras, Bill Sykes, alias Knify Jerk. He set out on his adventures in the most rigorous of weather. It was below zero to its most extreme depression. Even the Polar bears must have found it uncomfortably appetite-producing and keen. He had taken his bearings carefully, and all the other precautions which his recent overland experi- ences must have suggested. His nose was not quite such a classic member as he would have liked, if he had been at the fixing of it. His ears also were better developed than he exactly cared about. But, as Touchstone said about his poor spouse Audrey, they were his very own, and he had no notion of parting with them too suddenly. There- fore, he took good care to guard these members from frostbite. Besides the Indian sledge-drivers, who had trained their own deer and dogs to the work, he took with him a couple of half-breeds who had traversed the North-West territory froni the mouth of the Mackenzie to Hudson's Bay. The Indians were good for their own work, likewise for fishing and hunting; but these half-breeds, being cleaner in their habits, made more agreeable companions, At this time of the year, they had a dull kind of daylight for about six and a half hours. The trail which the gold piospectors had taken was almost east from Dawson City—was across country to the direction of the Franklin Lakes. Of course, he could not tell where they might have branched off, yet he trusted to his Indians and half-breeds to find out from some other native rovers.

One point Bill was sure about. This Lewis Eastlake and his partner must have covered a con- siderable tract of country, or they would have returned to Dawson City for the winter. Jack Wade he had heard described as a man who had spent many years prospecting in the East and North of the territory, and on the Peel and Red Rivers and their tributaries. It wasn't such a wild-goose chase as it may appear to readers who have not been in those regions. Prospectors who were daring enough to venture so far afield were scarce. The tribes of wandering Indians were to be met on the banks of rivers and streams, or in the woods and mountains, following the track of game. They were most likely to know all the whites who had passed through, and the direction they were taking. The wilds of Canqda are extensive, yet although a white man may run a hundred risks of death, from exposure, starvation, blizzards, treacherous rivers, falls down ice crevices, or the deadly hugging of grizzly bears, he can hardly cover his trail from those keen-eyed wanderers. . . .

In Canada, also, the Redskins are the trusting brothers of the British Palefaces. We have kept our treaties faithfully with them. They have the memory of broken pledges to make them abhor the American United Statesman as much as they respect

"You don't seem to have got cheerful news frora your despatches, mate ? " observed Bill Sykes, who had been watching him. "No, I have not; yet it is what I might have expected," replied the sick man with a forced sail bitter laugh. "A woman sold you, I guess. Yet if one Ds! done so there must be another who longs to repsg the injury." "What do you mean?" "Only that someone is mighty interested in • young fellow called Lewis Eastlake, and has 11 scarcity of spondulicks to prove that interest, replied Bill knowingly. "It can't be your mining partners, as they could easily get another man t° take your place. I have been commissioned V) look after you personally, as if you had been Sir John Franklin himself."

The thoughts of Sir Lewis turned gratefully tO the Duchess Valerie. She had not forgotten hilut if his wife had. "I can't say who the party is, as my instra: tions are vague, but I have to deliver you safe an° sound in Dawson City this summer, so set to fig fast as possible and get well." Scurvy is a trouble which is cured quickly taken in time. Bill Sykes had all the condiment" with him to effect a rapid cure, and very soon hill patient was on his feet and taking his share in the work. It had been late in the season before Jack Wadl and his partner had made this lucky discovery 11 the extreme end of the Yukon Valley. They 136._,a( fossicked and sunk Many a hole in different pan, with indifferent results before they had landeii this. But now it seemed they need go no further; the gold was here and in abundance. During the next two months they were all liar 3 at work, shovelling away snow and burning through the ice. They tested only a few panfunll to each hole, and dug these at the specified distant* from each claim to satisfy the Government rules. All were rich.

Bill Sykes decided on one claim, and each of big Indians and half-breeds chose theirs. There were a number of names affixed to others that Sir Lewitt was empowered to use for his London company, and, these pegged off and taken up, they were ready to go off to Klondike and announce the rich die' covery. "The Klondike Moose Mining Company° could now issue their prospectuses with a blare of trumpets.

They had discovered a new and immensely pro" fitable goldfield. ( To be continued.)

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 18, 1899.

A WARRINGTON TEAM WINS THE BOWLING CHAMPIONSHIP.

Over the well-kept green of the private bowling club at Whitetield, the teams of Wigan and Seuth Warrington met on Saturday afternoon to play off for the county president's prize enl the honour of holding toe county club championship of Lancashire and Cheshire for the next twelve months. Considerable interest was take e in the peiceedines, and bith sets of supporters were equally sanguine as to the issue of the match. The War- rinetoniane have always made a bold bid for honour in the competition, but during the past two years the main has been handicapped by the green at the B ill's Head having bec3fne so affected, as to not ale) wof a wood being trundled. That their position this year in the connluding tie has been gained by 55 esr merit c annot be doalred, and the several victories gained which landed then in such a preud position on Saturday have been tee outenne of rare pluck and d Teed pertinacity. The Whirefield green was seen at its beat and toe first quart- ette saw the VVarriagtonians gain a lead by ten shots.T Davies in his game with J Settle rarely left the issue in doubt,and although the Wiganer got two at the first end Davies with a succession of good woods established a strong lead, and the 22nd end of the game was called 21-9. NEGLECT OF CHILDREN AT SUTTON.

FATHER AND MOTHER SENT TO PRISON. "CHILDREN LIKE YOUNG ARABS."

Samuel Grindley, labourer, and his wife Mary Ann Grindley, of Gorsty Ho'low, Sutton, near Runcorn? Were again brought up before the magistrates at Runcorn Sessions, when the Bench was occupied by Sir Richard Brooke, Bart (chairman), and Messrs W. Timmins and J. Ockleston. Al on the previous occasion the defend- ants appeared the charge was the neglect of their six children in a manner likely to cause unnecessary suff-r- -hig and injury to their health on the 21st of July and ether days.—Mr. Knowles, solicitor, Widnes, prosecetad on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., whil,t the defendants con- ducted their own case.---Mr. Knowles reviewed the his- tory of the case, and stated that tee children now appeared in court fairly Ny dressed,and that was quite in keeping with his (Mr. Knowles') experience of such cases. The clothes were probably borrowed for the !occasion. He bad known one family stay In bed whilst Lhe others used the clothes to appear in Court.

Inspector Walter Hackett, stationed at Warrington, said the first proceedings in the case were taken by the police on the Ist May. Witness saw the boy Thomas about that time, and he was suffering from a burn wound which extended from tee knee to the groin. Tn.-) wound was raw, and in a very bad state indeed. The woman said he had had the wound a mantra, and she bad not seen a doctor about it. It was covered with a dirty piece of ray, and when this was removed the stench was something awful. The defendants subse- quently went for the doctor, and he told them to poul- tice the wound, and bring the child again. Instead of acting according to the doctor's advice tee mother bad continued to poultice the wound, and had made it worse. The doctor told her she would have to pay is 6 a visit, as she owed hirrt,a bill, but she did not go near him again. For over fifur months the wound was in a bad condition. On the 37)th June witness visited defen- dant's house, and again examined the child's leg. It was very painful, and when the dirty rag which covered it was removed, the child screamed frightfully. The child was very poorly clad. The boy George was lying on the parents' bed suffering from measles. He was filthy dirty, and was covered with a dirty sheet. The mother was picking coal. When the children caught sight of -witness coming they jumped through the window, and went and hid them- selves. They had always been half naked, and they looked like aeimals running about; more like little dirty Arab children than anything else. The only improve- ment witness observed was that defendants had two new quilts and two new sheets. The mattresses were rotten and filthy dirty. The floor in the children's room was filthy dirty with excrement in whieh the children had been paddling with bare feet. The bedstead was broken down, and the bed upon it was of such aimensions that it did not cover the laths. Some of the children would, therefore, have to sleep on the irons, and that, he sub- mitted, would entail suffering. The defendants had made no effort to improve this state of things. He bad telked to the woman about mending the condition of the house and children, and she told bim if it was not for such people as her his occupation would be gone. Last Friday the children were in a worse state altogether. He found them in a ditch, and they were soeking wet. Fred was dressed in a ragged jacket and a filthy dirty shirt, and was without shoes or stock- ings. On the left foot there was a wound about an inch and half long, caused, the boy said, by treading on an , old nail in a'fence. There was also a wound on the boy's shin, which, his mother said, was caused by being run over by a cart wheel. It was in a very bad state, and the boy was walking on his toes. George was very poorly clad, and had sores on his feet. Witness told the defendant she would have to be brought before the court, and she replied the sooner it came about the bet- ter, as they would keep her in gaol, and there would be less expense in the house. The defendants had had no- tice to leave the house. The children had vermin bites upon them. Sergeant Bourne, lately stationed at Sutton Weaver, gave corroborative evidence. The farmers were annoyed by the children,who ran about wild. Defendant was a farm labourer [lt was here stated that defendant was in receipt of £1 per week, and one boy earned 6s a week].—Sergeant Bourne said the children had been got up for the occasion. If they saw them three days hence they would not know them. The male (defendant :said it was a queer job if the children could be made so clean and tidy in three days. The people were saying it was a scandalous skame to bring them to the court. Sergeant Bourne said the boot was on the other leg. The neighbours were of opinion that the case ought to have been taken years ago. The male defendant said he could do no more than he had done, even if they sent him to prison. They were just making a start now. He had had 23 children and he had never had a doctor to them. They did not look as if they needed one. Mr. Ockleston : Did your wife tell you that I said if she had been in Liverpool she would have got six months for the child's leg P—Defendant : No.—The female defendant: Yes, I told you that.—Defendant: She may have told me. I don't take notice of such thiogs.—Mr. Ocklestown • That's it. Mary Ann Grindley, the female defendant, on being sworn stated that she told the inspector she bought two pairs of sheets and two quilts. She had not neg- lected the children. They had got notice to leave the house through this case.—Cross-examined by Mr. Knowles: Her husband was earning £1 and a boy 6s per:week. It never occurred to her to go to the reliev- ing officer for a doctor's note. She was too proud to do that. Dr. Barton did not speak kindly to her. Witness did not take beer.—Mr. Knowles : Is not your husband a regular whiskey drinker ?—Witness : No, he may have a little. He worked at Frodsham for Miss Andrews. —Sergeant Browne said the husband drank at the week-end. He saw him on one occasion going home at 11.50 p.m. ins drunken state. For the last two or three months be bad been at heme every night. After viewing the child Thomas's leg the Chairman said they were convinced that the ease had been made out. The defendants appeared to bevelled every chance, and it was non the first time they had been there. The defend nt, Samuel Grindley, would be sent to prison for one month with hard laheur, and Mary Ann Grind- ley would have to go for three months' hard labour.

212 209 A majority of three for Warrington. THE FILEsMITHS' STRIKE AT WAR RINGTON.

AN ABORTIVE ATTENIPT AT SE TTLEMEN T.

We give below copies of letters which have passed between Messrs. Stubs, Limited, and the Secretary of the Filesmiths' Strike Committee. It will be seen that the firm declined to discuss the situation with the strike committee, but expressed their eadiness to receive a deputation consisting of their own men. A conference was arranged,and to9l4lace last Saturday, week, but unfortunately no agree- ment was arrived at. The representatives of the firm, we understand, refused to make any conces- sions whatever, and expressed their determination not to give way. The men on the other hand are' equally determined not to return .to work under the old conditions, which they contend, do not permit of a living wage being earned. They are however, quite willing to submit their case to arbi- tration or comply with any other means which may be devised with the object of bringing the dispute to a conclusion, satisfact n'y to both parties. The men are now receiving strike pay weekly, and a great number of contributions have been received from various trade organisations. A strong appeal has been made on behalf of the filesmiths to rail- way workers 'oy the "Railway Review" under the heading" Help the Fighting Filesmitbs." The correspondence referred to is as follows : " Filesmiths' Association. 22, Cartwright-street, Warrington, August 2, 1899. Re Strike.—Messrs. Stubs, Limited, Gentlemen. It having come to our knowledge that you are desirous of receiving a deputation in connection with the unfortunate dis- pute now existing between us, I am directed . to inform you that the Committee who are conducting the strike would have no objection to meet you in conference, so that a settlement could be arrived at satisfactory to all concerned. Yours obediently JAMES BATTERSBY, secretary." The followitu reply was received :—"Warrington August 3rd, 1899, Mr. James Battersby, secretary, Filesmith's Association, 22, Cartwright-street War- rington, Sir,—We are in receipt of your letter of yesterday in which you say that you'are directed to inform us that the committee who are conducting the strike would have no objection to meet us in conference so that a settlement could be arrived at satisfactory to all concerned ' In reply we are al- ways willing to go into any matter needing discus- :ion with our own men and are ready to receive a deputation from them, but must decline to discuss matters with a committee of any but our own men. Yours truly, Peter Stubs, Limited, F. A. Frost, managing director.' A deputation of Nfesers Stub's men waited upon ropresentatives of the firm at eleven o'clock last Saturday week: with the result already stated.

AN INT.E REST fNG LICENSING CASE AT WARRINGTON.

THE LICENSEE OF THE WHITE BULL REFUSES

TO LEAVE A very interesting licensing application was made to the borough magistrates on Monday morning, \lr. F. Monks (in the chair). Mr. Browne, OR behalf of Messrs. Greenali, Whitley and Company, applied for temporary authority to be granted to Thomas Edward Brumbell, to sell at the White Bull, Horse narket-street now kept by Mrs. Anne Balshaw. He said he understood that the application was to be opposed, and he would, therefore, defer what he had to say until he heard what Mr. RoloPrts had to urge.

Mr. F. R. Rob rr..ts said he appeared on behalf of Mrs. Balshaw. the present licensee, ta object to temporary authority being granted, as his client was still the holder of the license, and three months had yet to run. Until Messrs. Greonall and Whitley had come to some arrangement with her she declined to go out. He sug- gested that the came should b 3 adjourned for a week, although he did not know whether the Bench had power to order that to be done. _ _

Alderman Sutton : Are the parties here ? M. Roberts replied that Mrs. B ashaw was absent, as she had been forbidden by her doctor to attend. The interests of his client would be jeopardised if she gave up possession of the property, and they did not know what position they would be in. Mr. Browne said he had been instructed by Mr. Down personally to say that Mrs. Balshaw would be treated in the most liberal manner if she wonld go out. If, however, she did not do so, a writ of ejectment would be applied for on the following day. Messrs. Greenall and Company had been compelled to gve her notice although she had lo,±en in the house 23 years. They had come to Court very reluctantly to apply for temporary authority. The question of monetary matters was one for a civil court. Tney were prepared to take over the spirits and stock, and pay for all trade fixtures. Mr. Robnts thought that some slight misunderstand- ing existed, as three months had yet to run. Mrs. Bal- shaw was entitled to hold the license for a further three months. They were not trying the question ot agree- ment in that Court.

Alderman Sutton: If the covenants are broken the agreement will came altogether. What i 3 the use of retaining the license? Mr. Browne: Messrs. Greenall do not give notice without a cause.

Mr. RoberLs : My client is still the licensee,and there are three months to run. She has not been able to come to terms with Messrs. Greenall, and she, therefore, obj•cts to abandon the licenss. Councillor Snaw Green : Who is now in posses 310 n of the license? _ _

Mr. Raberts : Mrs. Btlshaw. The question of eject- ment is one for another court. You cannot take away the license unless you object to her character. The Chairmtn : I fancy Messrs. Greens.ll and Com- pany have some reason for giving her notice. Mr. Browne : It will pay Mrs. BAMaw far better to do as we wish, becauss we are not bound to buy the spirits and stock or fixtures. She can take them out. Councillor Shaw Green : Adjourn the ease for an hour.

Mr. Roblrts : We must have an undertaking in wri ting. Mr. Browne We won't give one. If Mr. Down's word is not go xi, nothing is god. We have offered a very handsome and reasonable sum. She is claiming for what happened 23 years ago, belor3 we were the awners, and when the house belonged to the late Mr. Alderman Thpherd. We don't know whether it is a right or just claim. We have instructed Mr. Owen to Valll9 the fixtures.

_ Mr. Roberts : I dispute that statement utterly. Why should Messrs. Greenall and Company objmt to give us a statement in writing? The Chairman : We don't want to go into that m atter in open court, and we suggest, therefore, that you should endeavour to come to some arrangement. It will be well for Mr. Roberts to give consideration to the matter.

Mr. Roberts : We don't know all the facts. The Chairman : If it is necessary for us to go into ;hem we will do so. Mr. Roberti: You cannot do so.

The Chairman: lam sure you (Mr. Roberts) will be consulting the interests of your client by a further con- ference with Mr. Browne.

The parties then left the Court for a consultation, and on their return in abut three-quarters of an hour. Mr. Browne said : Terms have been arrived at. We have simply undertaken to do what we did on Saturday, and, therefore, I apply for temporary authority. caMr. R )berts : And I withdraw the apposition, be- use we have an undertaking in writing and are quite satisfied. The application was then ranted. CLOG DA.NOING.

BOY CEIAMPION INFERVIEWED.

IN Durham many years ago, resided a youth named George Temperley, celebrated tor his clever exhibitions in clog-dancing. At six yoars of age he was the Cham- pion Boy Clog-Dancer, and his exhibitions graatly de- lighted the miners. At the age of sixteen he went to sea. In March of the present year he arrived at Ushaw Moor, a village a few miles from Durham city, whore his father ably discharges the duties of landlord of the Flass Inn, a popular resort for miners. It was here that a representative of the 6' Durham Chronicle " found him and the story he related was, to say the least, remark- able. _

Mr. George Temperley, now a young man of 24 years of age, said, "As a youth I was robust and healthy, and until I was sixteen years of age ailed very little. Then my troubles began, and since then I have never been toe same. At that time I determined to become a sea-going engineer. On my return journey trom Balti- more to Bristol the weather was very rough indeed, and owing to being wet through and sleeping in my wet clothes, I caught rheumatic fever. The next voyage we were bound for Marseilles, but on arriving there I was compelled to leave the ship and return home. I was then very ill, and could not walk about. I was also suffering severely from eczema." Continuing, Mr. George Temperley said that he is 50 per cent, better now than he was in March. " And to what do you attribute your cure ?" queried the " Durham Chronicle " man. _ _

"To Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for pale people. Since I started to take them in March I have gone on improv- ing,. and now lam almost recovered from eczema ; as for the rhematism, it has now left me entirely." Migs Jennie Temperley, sister to Mr. George Temper- ley, then araciously consented to add her testimony to the gool that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills bad wroug,bt in her own case.

Miss Temperley said, " My father read in the news- papers aboit the wonderful cures that had been effected by Dr. Williams' pink pills, and proposed that we should give them a trial. Accordingly three months aga I commenced to take the pills. I had been suffering from anemia for a long time past, and nothing seamed to do me any good. " When I had taken three boxes of Dr. Williams' pink pills, however, I felt my strength coming back gradu- ally, and very soon after, I felt quite different alto- gether, and I attribute my recovery entirely to Dr. Williams' pink pills." Acting, as they do, upon the blood, it is natural that in Dr. Williams' pink pills should be found a remedy both for ante aria, eczema, rheumatism, and the other diseases which arise trim one form or another of im- poverished blood. Rheumatic fever,liks influenz a,almost always leaves some other disorder b9hinl it—toe often heart weakness. Dr. Williams' pink pills, by their strengthening Kfect on the nPrvea and their unique power of enriching the blood, fortify the system against attack by disease. Tway are unrivalled as a tonic, and as a remedy for the debiliting of hot relaxing summer weather. They have cured many other ailments—de- bility, St. Vitus' dance, comsumption, and paralysis. They may be obtained post free at two shillings and ninepence a box, or thirteen and nine for six boxes, from Dr.Williams' Medicine Company,Elolborn Viaduct, London.

GOOD TEMPLARS' CONVENTION.

The half-yearly meeting of the No 1. Convention, S.W. Lancashire Good Templars, was held in the Cave Irown Institute. Ashton, on Saturday. The meeting opened at 3.49 p.m., Bro John Vint, C Co., presiding. There was a good attendance of officers and representatives from lodges at Warrington, Earlestown, Sutton, St. Helens, Elaydock, Ashton, Wigan, Hindley, Leigh, &c. Bro W E*atts, P.D. A.Sec. of Liverpool, was heartily received. Bros J Woods and P Bentham were appointed to report on credentials, and a good selection was made for re- porting to the press the proceedings of the Con- vention. Bro E Newman, C Sec., reported on the numerical position of the lodges in the convention area, which showed a slight decrease. The lodges generally were carrying on active outdoor mission work ; especially St. Helens, Earlestown, Ashton, Wigan, &c. Bro George Clarke, C Treasurer,showed a satisfactory balance in hand. Sister Mrs Wools, C.S.J.T., gave a most encouraging report, on the juvenile temples which indicated general activity. She had visited every temple in the area during her term of office, and the figures gave 573 members in , nine temples, increase 47 on the quarter. Electoral matters received particular atten- tion and consideration ; it was reported that active opposition was being arranged for in various parts of the area, to applications for new licenses at coming Brewster Sessions, The election af officers next took place, resulting as follows : C.C.T., Bro A Francis, St. Helens; C. Co., Bro John Vint, Ashton; C.S.J.T. Sister Mrs Woods, Earlestown; C.E. Superintendent Bro. Turner, St. Helens; C V.T. Sister Mrs "Vint, Ashton; C. Sec., Bro. E. Newman, Earlestown; C. Chap. Bro. Rev T E Stead ; Brvnn ; C. Mar, Bros. Joseph Sheath, Warriegton ; P.C.C.T., Bro J W Roberts, Warrington. The next monthly Session was fixed for Widnes on September 9. Bro Evans, of Liver- pool, and several othera gave short encouraging addresses. Thenks to Bro Evans, and to the Ash- ton brethren for their arrangements closed the Ses- sion about 5.45, when a well-attended public tea was done full justice to. An open air meeting was held afterwards near Ashton Church. Bra A Francis, P.D.S.J.T. presided over an exellent audi- ence, which manifested a deep interest in the variOtt9 itams. Earnest addresses were given by Bro Rev T E Stead, C.. Chap, Bro George Clarke, D.E.S., Bro J Woods, L.D., and Bro Evans, who also rendered a temperance melody very accept ibly. Songs, recitations, &c., were given by members of the Juvenile Temple. A successful session was brought to a close about 9 p.m.

WARRINGTON.

PERSONAL.

It appears from a statement which has been pre- pared of the votes and attendances of Lancashire members of Parliament, that Mr. It. Pierpoint, M.P., is eredited with the high figures of 202. There were only seven other Lancashire members who made more attendances. THE VOTERS' LISTS.

The list of voters has now been issued and copies will be found attached to the doors of places of worship in the town. All Liberals are requested to examine the lists for themselves and see if their names are included. In case of mis-description of name, place of abode, or qualification they should at once see Mr. Clayton at the Liberal Club. All lodgers occupying rooms of the value of £lO and upwards since the 15th of July last year are eutitled to a vote. It is important that the lists should be as accurate as possible especially in view of the possibility of a general election next year. PICNIC TO PICKNIERS LAKE. _ _ _

On Saturday, Mr. W. Brough's Society Class had their annual outing to the above place in beauti- fully fine weather. No fewer than 86 members and 15 invited friends took advantage of the fine day and enjoyed a splendid drive. The party left Sankey Bridges at 1.30 p.m. arriving at Pickmera at 3.40. Tea was got ready at once and Mr.Brough's hospitality was greatly appreciated. After tea, the members and friends made their way to the large lake and enjoyed themselves by rowing in the boats. Eight o'clock being the time appointed to start back, Lhe members met at Mrs. Riley's house were tea had been provided. After a pleasant journey Sankey Bridges was reached about 10.15, all feeling very grateful to Mr. W. Broligh for his kindness towards them. ALLEGED PUBLIC-HOTISE THEFT.

Before the -Mayor (Alderman Wareing) and Alderman Sadler, at the Widnes Police Court, on Monday, Catherine Matthews, of no fixed aboad, was charged with stealing between 7.30 and 9 o'clock, on the evening of the sth August, a pair of trousers and a shirt, belonging to Thomas Drury. The Cbief Constable said Drury was a farm labourer at Penketh. He redeemed the articles on the night in question from Bred's pawnshop, in Waterloo-road. He afterwards went to the Britannia Inn to have a drink. Re there met prisoner, onc. after a few words went out, leaving the package on the farm. On his return be found both the woman and the articles missing. He went back to the shop, and told the manager of what had happened. About nine o'clock prisoner went to the pawnshop, and asked 3s. 61. on the articles. The manager's sus- picion being aroused, he made inquiries, and the police were sent for, and the woman given into the custody of Police-constable Falkingham. When charged, she had nothing to say. Prisoner was committed to the next Liverpool County Inter- mediate Sessions. FIRE IN BACK DALLIM-LANE. On Saturday night, about eight o'clock Mr. It. Wood, 57,- Bewsey-street, noticed smoke issuing from the roof of a cabinet workshop tenanted by Messrs. Regenbogen, in Back Dallam-lane. Being apprehensive that the place was on fire, he called to a neighbour and proceeded to the spot. By this time the smoke was coming through the slates in greater volume, and looking through the keyhole the flooring was seen to be on fire. A lurry from the forge passing at the moment, an iron crowbar was procured and the door was forcibly opened. Meanwhile information was sent to the occupiers. When an entrance was effected dense volumes of smoke issued forth from the flooring and woodwork appertaining to the trade, which was on fire. Buckets of water were speedily procured and the flames extinguished. The place adjoining is utilized as a store room for paper, and had it not been for the promptitude displayed by Mr. Wood a serious conflagration would have resulted. The cause of the fire is unknown. THE SILT TRADE OF THE UNITED KINGDOM. 'llls official returns of the salt dealt with by all the centres of the kingdom disclose the fact that during July there was a falling off in coastwise and export shipments of 2412 tons. The total quantity shipped was 90,347 tons, of which Liver- pool had the advantage of 47,274 tons, and Run- , corn, Weston, and the Manchester Ship Canal 10,852 tons. The Mersev city despatched 12,905 tons in excess of July, 1898, but the other Mersey ports were depressed to the extent of 8790 tons. Liverpool's improvement was due to increased de- mands from Asia and the United States, while thl!, falling off at Runcorn and the Manchester Ship Canal resulted in a lesser demand from Scotland and a reduction in the trade with British North America to the extent of 3000 tons. With the exception of Fleetwood,which has lost 3650 tons, and Middles- borough, 3476 tons, the ether districts have been normal. So far this year 619,113 tons of salt have been shipped, this being• 6280 in advance of the same months of 1898. Have you seen the NEW PORK SHOP that J. BATLEY ISAAC has opened in Horsemarket-street.—Advt A WARRINGTON MIN FOUND DROWNED.— INQUEST.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inqusst at the Borough Police Station,on Saturday after- noon. on the body of Frederick William Fox, who was found in the river Mer6ey,near Bishop's Wharf, on Friday morning.—James Fox, 130, 13attersby- lane, Warrington, a goods guard, in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company. identified the deceased as his son by the clothing. Deceased had on a belt known as a cholera belt which he wore when he was in the army, and wit- ness saw deceased's nucr ber on the belt. Recently the deceased finished his time with the Grenadier guards, having served three years. He was a labourer, 21 years of age, and he lived with them. He saw him last on Bank Holiday about Inoon. He could not believe he had taken his life, or that there had been any foul play, as he wee well known and respected in Warrington. He had a good situation to go to.—lnspector Phillips said deceased came to him about three weeks ago, and asked for a situation in the police force.—Thomas Curtis, 15, Orchard-street, stated that on Monday evening he took deceased towards home, but when they were near the Manx Arms he said he would go by himself the rest of the way, and he went on. Deceased had had some drink.—A crane driver at the Bishop's Wharf named Joseph Mercer, said he saw the body of the deceased in the river near the wharf on Friday morning, and information was given to the police.—A verdict oi"Found drowned" was returned.

SETTING a trap for the A Shilling Duty, benevolent is never a wise Noceeuing, and the Protec- tionists who have fixed so many traps lately, must not be astonished if any device of this kind in connection with old-age pensions is regarded with special distrust. Mr. CHAPLIN has been diseourr ing upon old-age pensions and a shilling duty no corn, and hence we may fairly consider that the proposition is a feeler. The registratioa dutY would yield about two millions of money. Of course, we are assured that the re-imposition Of this duty would not raise the price of corn or et bread and flour. We may be excused a reasonable scepticism on this head. Mr. CHAPLIN declares that when the duty was taken off it did not affect the price of wheat. That may be, because all reductions do not take effect at once, and this one was appropriated by the wheat buyers and the floursellers and bakers. But au increase in price always tells at once end that is the point to be kept well in mind. The consumer is seldom allowed the advantage or stocks purchased at a reduced price, and if e shilling a quarter duty were placed upon foreige corn, which is now in hardly the SAME, proportion to the total comumed as it was when Mr. Lowg took off the duty, the price of broad and flour would be sure to be affected. The consumption ef foreign corn in this country is now between BO and six million quarters more per year than it Wes when Mr. LOWE took off the duty. There will he a great and natural reluctance to placing any la' post on food, and especially upon what has beer' called the staff of life, because bread forms s° large a part of the food of the working classes. It would be a reactionary policy, and, as such, would be resisted to the utmost. It would be so indirect contribution by the wage-earners to the pension they were to receive, and it would affect the poorest of men most. The richer and the higher classes do not consume much bread,and the cern-duty would, relatively, affect them very little. The suggestion, moreover, is untimely. If the Government desires to give pensions to the aged and deserving poor it should not dream of get' ling the money by taxation of this kind, likely enough to grow with the extensioo of the scheme. If the shilling duty would pro' duce two millions for a small beginning, tw° shillings would double it, and four shillings would furnish as much money as is ever likely to be re' quired. It would be so easy to 4rgue that it woUld be better to increase the duty than to find song° other tax, when more money would be required, es it certainly would be in the course of a few years: Public discussion will soon convince the doubtft.,l3 that a bread tax, however small, for the purpose of obtaining funds for an old-age pension schen)* will never do at all. It is not necessary to sugge4 an alternative plan of raising the money. Finding the money is the duty of the Statesmen who pro" pound the scheme, and if any new taxation be re" quired it should fall upon;the persons best able te bear it, and not upon a poor class already paying more to the State than its earnings justify. ref: haps it is fortunate that Mr. CHAPLIN has so earl/ in the recess indicated the danger which beset° us—namely, a trap for the benevolent. Baia mildly Protectionist,in order to be mildly humsee' is a policy that will not answer and ought net t° answer.

They were necessary and we now see that they Were ineffective rather than too elaborate, The trial will be prolonged by the incident we are dis- cussing, and that will increase the time of danger and :promote the schemes of the conspirators.
