The Newton Urban District Council seems to be on the horns of a dilemma in consequence of the action of the Local Government Board in refusing to sanction loans for private street improvement purposes until efficient sewage disposal works have been provided for the district. On the face of it it does seem rather hard upon the inhabitants that because their local authority has failed to provide sewage works to the satisfaction of the Local Government Board they must also be com- pelled to experience the discomforts of unpaved streets. We do not know what steps the Newton Council purpose taking to place their sewage sys- tem on a more up-to-date basis. The out- side public has hoard very little about the matter since the Inspector of the Local Government Board condemned the Council's present means of disposing of sewage. It is certainly time some active measures were taken in the matter, for the present impasse is intolc. e, and is a grave re- Section on any self-res, .ng local body. The efficient treatment of so -ge h-s now passed the experimental stage, and the members of the Nu w- ton authority are able te profit by the experience of others. The bacteriological treatment of sew- age, for instance, is now being worked with great success at Blackburn and other places. There is no getting away from the fact that it will be an expensive matter, but the sooner the Newton Coun- cil determines to face the difficulty, the better it will be for all concerned.

The Vicar of Burtonwood supplies his parish- ioners through the medium of his "Church and Parish Chronicle" with monthly dis erta tions on various subjects. After exhausting all parochial topics he proceeds further afield and expresses opinions which must even surprise the people of this Colliery Settlement. For instance in this month's issue he draws lessens from the anomal- ous position of the Episcopalians in Scotland where the Established Church is "Prestbyterian." Then he proceeds to refer to his progress in the paths of Vegetarianism and raps the knuckles of traducers. To add a little further variety, "Nun- quam's" views on Education are carefully analysed; and the hanging of Mary Ansel' for the murder of her mad sister provides a topic on which to express the opinion that "the execution of the poor halt-witted girl was an act of legalized brutality." But one of the most interesting con- tributions is that referring to Workmen's Baths. He bemoans the fact that no baths are provided at Burtonwood where the hard-working collier is unable to wash his "black and grimy" body. Most people will be in sympathy with the Vicar in endeavouring to bring about the introduction of baths at BurtonwoDd but when he begins to talk of baths paying a good return in interest on the capital invested he seems to confirm the opinion that parsons are bad business man. It is a well- known fact that baths are not " paying " concerns and this is confirmed by the balance sheets of municipalities. In the last return of the Borough Treasurer of Warrington we find that the baths in Legh-street cost the ratepayers £495 9s 6d despite the fact that £463 16s were paid by bathers for admission. We would recommend the Vicar to induce the local Parish Council to take up the Baths and Workhouses Act in the interests of the public health; but if he endeavours to introduce baths under the belief that they will" pay" we are afraid the ratepayers.will find that he is a false prophet.

One point which was clearly brought out in the recent discussions on the commercial competition to which we are now subjected was the lack of industrial literature and technical information from which our manufacturers suffer, in striking; contrast to some of our better-informed competi- tors. In the United States, for instance, the efficiency of its trade and technical publications has been a factor in the country's great commercial success. Great Britain on the ether hand has struggled on without such assistance, but the reproach, we are glad to record, is now being removed. Wo have received a copy of the first issue of " Feilden's Magazine" which is to furnish a record of the world's progress, and deal more especially with everything that is best in British engineering practice and produc- tions. The letter-press and illustrations are excellent; the quality of the work being a distinct feature of the magazine. In these days when magazines seem to be produce.l with the one idea of giving the greatest bulk for the smallest price, it is pleasing to note that Mr. Feilden has spared no expense on the production of this maga- zine, which will compare favourably with the best work to be found in the leading printing trade journals that make a speciality of fine typo- graphy. The magazine contains carefully pre- pared articles,copiously illustrated by photographs and diagrams. They are written by the highest authorities of the day on various branches of en- gineering science and cannot fail to be of great value to all engaged in industrial activity. It con- tains just the information wanted, and no manu- facturer who is desirous of keeping abreast of the times can afford to be without it.

A succession of summers like the on through which we are passing would make a revolution in our dresses, dietaries, and habits. We seem to have got a taste of what summer is like in other countries, but we have made no startling changes, owing to crass conservatism,which gives us the top hat and the stand-up collar, the many skirts of the ladies, and their heavy garments. Three months real summer would cause a revolution. We should be wearing pith helmets and canvas shoes, white coats and trousers, as some of us did in our younger days. Workmen would not wear dark cloth clothes in stuffy weather, but light-coloured washing clothes. Ladies would be airy, gossamer creatures, gaudy as butterflies. It would be a delicious change. But it is most unlikely. Next summer it may be cold and wet, and the philosophy we have been storing up in our heats and langucrs may all be dissipated. It is the same with a cold winter when it comes. It seems to knock the bottom out of everything. Pipes freeze, cisterns burst, fires will not warm rooms, gas goes out, streets are impassable, and our lan- guage has in it a mixture of Hindustani and Scotch in about equal proportions. Everybody has dreams of furs. The average Englishman laughs at these sudden ups and downs. We are a middling country, and so we pall remain. We Shall not build houses with a tempcirature under freezing of Scandinavian mortar,nor construct any dwellings, nor reform our garments. A cold sum- rner will set us up, and a warm winter will let us down. Hence our perennial subject is the weather. Arid when you come to be philosophical there is real sense in the saying, " Uod willing and weather Permitting !"

The magistrates at the Warrington Borough Police Court, like other people, have difficulties to contend with. But the particular one which we desire to illustrate is that of prosecuting a husband f ter the wife has obtained a free warrant against ner spouse for assault. In most cases the wife

===== 0002601_18990818_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
appears before the Bench after the conclusion of a "Court" in a tearful state bearing self-evident marks of abuse. If there is good reason for sup- posing the case a deserving one the application is, of course, granted. The wife at this time appears to see her husband's faults as they are and her assertions that she will proceed with the prosecution to the end seem convincing enough, despite the scepticism which the magistrates and Chief Constable display through long experience with such cases. Too often their reason has proved the soundness of its basis at the expense of their judgment. Lately we have had a plethora:of such cases and the magistrates had reason for the in- dignation they expressed against a complainant in a case on Tuesday. The circumstances of the one in question were not uncommon but the wife's de- liberate mis-statement of the facts were so glar inglyi apparent as to call forth strong rernatks from the bench. When asking for a free warrant or summons the complainant must make her statement on oath and when the evidence at the hearing of the case,also on oath,is in direct opposi- tion the matter becomes serious. There will be the forgiving wife so long as the world lasts and as such, she will ever command sympathy, but surely such extremities are hardly necessary.

The payment in wages in kind has long since gone out of fashion in most parts of the world, for the simple reason that currency of some sort exists in nearly every country, unless it be in parts of Africa. Beads and cotton stuffs are still used in some parts to pay the wages of carriers. But in States under British protection we should hardly expect to meet with what is recorded in a Government report about Nyasaland, otherwise known as the Central Africa Protectorate. In parts of this State labourers are paid in pieces of calico, and they do not seem to like the method at all. The calico wage system disinclines them to stay in the region, and our commissioner re- ports that if the labourers are not paid better wages they will soon have all migrated to lands where currency is employed. Nyasaland runs like a promontory along the top north-east border of Rhodesia. In Rhodesia they pay wages on a cur- rency system, and the calico plan is unknown. The Nyasa labourers are accordingly making for this happier land as quickly as they can. During the first three months of this year one thousand labourers obtained permits and left Nyasaland for Mashonaland. There is railway wo:k there, and work in mines and on farms can be had. The news has spread, and the dusky Nyasans are leav- ing behind them the calico system for something more reasonable. Even were strips of calico given as wages they would be paid at a higher rate in Mashonaland. In the workings of the human mind calico has never before played such an inter- esting part.

The newspaper reporter's duty is not always clearly defined: sometimes it is plain enough and at other times his course of action should be dictated by his commonsense. We all know that primarily he is the servant of those who engage him, but it is also a recognized fact that the public have a strong claim on his services. Per- haps it is unwise to make this distinction as from the fact of fulfilling his duty by his newspaper employers the reporter is made a public servant. However this may come about he is, from the very nature of his public duties, looked upon more as an official. some members of the Warrington Rural District Council seem, temporarily at least, to have lost sight of these facts. What used to happen occasionally is now becoming customary. The press representatives are now beginning to expect to hear the remark at each meeting: "There is no further necessity for the reporters to stay" or "The press may now retire.' Though the Chair- man usually voices these quasi orders it is assumed from the silence of the members that such remarks are with their consent. We presume that the members of the Warrington Rural District Coun- cil are aware that they are public servants, and as such their meetings, unless in exceptional cases, are open to the" press "for the enlightenment of the public. They seem to have forgotten this fact. We wish to be clearly understood on this point. We on our part are quite aware that it is not always advisable for the "press" to be present but such occasions are getting too fre- quent,and this thought must have occurred to Mr. Thoraton:on Wednesday when he protested against the Chairman's " request " to the press representa- tives. Mr. Thornton said he did not believe in "smuggling these disc•issions," and as Mr. Thorn- ton takes part in the " discussions" his opinion should be worth having. The point was put to the vote,the result being that the reporters had to retire. We hope these occasions will be less fre- quent in future.

Another encouraging record of progress was laid before the shareholders assembled at the general meeting of the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany held last Thursday. There is an increased revenue of £13,126 compared with the correspond- ing half of last year,and it is believed that with the profits of the current half year the company will be able to complete the payment of a sum of I:50,000 to the Manchester Corporation for inter- est. There was no criticism of the able statement made by the Chairman, Mr. J. K. Bytheil, the shareholders appearing well satisfied with the in- creasing prosperity of the concern. It is worth while pointing out, however, that the rate of crease has not been quite maintained. During the first part of 1893 the volume of traffic and revenue was increasing by such leaps anA bounds as to raise hopes of a s'Anewhat extravagant nature in the breasts of some of the original shareholders— and in fact had the increase been maintained at the same rate there is no telling what I might have become possible—but the prospect was I too good to be true. Though there has been a I continuous forward movement in the affairs of the Company, the rate of progress has slightly diminished, which for a new, enterprising, and well managed concern is not entirely satisfactory. Ports as old as civilization almost are still adding to:their commercial returns, and it is therefore to be expected that Manchester, the healthy infant port, will not only grow but move along the road to success with an ever-lengthening stride. There are explanations for the slight retardation in the rate of progress, which Mr. Bythell explained to the general satisfaction, though it would have been more gratifying had no explanations been neces- sary. But there is nothing to be pessimistic about in the affairs of the Canal. The outlook is decidedly hopeful, and when the energetic management has succeeded in overcoming preju- dices and breaking fresh ground, the volume of traffic will surely and rapidly be enlarged. There is plenty of room for development, particu- larly in the direction of commerce with Asia, Africa, and South America—Lancashire's most productive trading grounds—and we feel convinced ' that ere long the Ship Canal will be playing .;.s proper part in the trade with these impel:taut countries. Farther signs of prosperity ave easily discovered in the slow but sure establishment of great works and manufactories on the banks of the waterway, and in the rapid expansion at the docks necessitated by increasing requirements.

We notice in the Engineer's report as to works, that" the quantities of sewage matter brought down by the River Mersey have been distinctly increased. The foul condition of that river is at present very marked." Mr. Hunter might as well have added that the Ship Canal itself was not one whit the better, and then perhaps the statement would have attracted the attention it deserves. So far it has been passed over in silence but we should like to know how it is that the quantities of sewage "have been distinctly increased." The Mersey and Irwell Joint Committee is supposed to pay strict attention to the pollution of the waters under its charge, but it cannot have exercised the amount of vigilance necessary or . there would be no such statement from the engineer of the Ship Canal. This increasing foulness of water flowing through such thickly-populated districts is a serious matter for the public health. The long spell of kot weather we have had this summer has made the impurity of the Mersey and Ship Canal very apparent to those whose olfactory nerves are the least bit sensitive. We have become so accustomed to the name of the Warrington and District Mare and Foal Society that it seems quite strange to speak of it as an Agricultural Society. The new name has been necessary in consequence of extending the scope and usefulness of the society by including amongst the exhibits farm stock and agricultural produceigenerally. Last week's show was the best which has yet been held in connection with the Society, the progress of former years being well maintained. The new classes added further 'interest to the show and there seems ground for the belief that the beneficial result which has followed the work of the Society in improving the breed of horses in this neighbour- hood will ultimately be extended to cattle and the other classes which have been introduced. Con- sidering the situation of Warrington, it is some- what surprising that such a Society should only have been in existence for ten years. But now that Warrington has entered the lists in real , earnest we have sufficient local patriotism to believe that the promoters of the Warrington Agricultural Society will not allow their annual show to occupy a secondary position.

There is a trite saying that lookers on see most of a garde. The following incident is a case in point. On one of our suburban 'buses the other day, the conductor, a callow youth, went inside, to collect the fares,and having relieved the passengers on one side of the necessary copper, placed himself before a young lady on the other side. The latter tendered a sixpenny piece to the conductor and whilst he was engaged in finding change the vehicle gave a sudden lurch, and to save himself the conductot made a wild grab for the strap or rod overhead, plunging the lady at the same time into a paroxysm of pain by treading on her toe. The look of exquisite agony on her face was sufficient indication to the observer that the conductor's boot had come in contact with the übiquitous corn. The young man in the meantime had recovered his equilibrium and the change and in happy ignorance of what had occurred proceeded to hand the latter to his fair passenger. The lady had now recovered herself sufficiently to flash a look of indignation at the conductor, who seeing no reason for this, returned it with a haughty glance leaving her aghast at what she deemed his effrontery. Two passengers at least, who noticed this little comedy, continued the journey while struggling manfully to conceal their mirth from the sufferer.

FOUR SHILLINGS A WEEK FOR Two FINGERS.

Through the kindness of the friends at Camden, the Nelson-street Wesleyan Sabbath Schools,lield their anni- versary in that capacious building, and both morning and evening good congregations assembled. The preacher was the Rev. T. Wilkes, of Southport (form- erly of this circuit) and he founded his able discourse upon the Revelation of St. John tire Divine,2lst chapter 7 verse : " He that overcome% shall inherit all thing, and I will be his God arid he shall be My son." In the afternoon Mr. B.Brown, of Widnes, who had promised to conduct the service, sent an anology ; and a very efficient substitute was found in Mr. Robinson, of Wid- nes, who delivered an excellent address on "The growth of Methodism from the timeof John Wesley," which was very attentively listened to throughout by a large con- gregatien. At the evening service the Runcorn Orches- tral Society censiderably augmented the musical por- tion of the service by their valuable assistance, under the conductorship of Mr. Horace Handley. The efficient organist was Mr. J. W. Temperley, Lewlands-road, and the c:onductor of the festival was Mr. Samuel Baz'ev, who it should he stated was responsible for the very efficient training of the choir and children in the pretty music sung through the anniversarv.—The number of scholars on the honks is 130 with about 14 teachers and officers, the superintendents being Messrs. G. Bayley and W. Lambert. The collections on behalf of the scholars realised £l9 which is an advance upon that of last year.—Consideted upon the whole the festival has been the most s ccessful within recent years, and re- flects highly upon those responsible for its conduct.

THE EPIDEMIC AT HELSBY.

Dr. Alfred Curtis, on b.half of Dr. Adams, reported : " Sinn Dr. Adams left for his holidays, thee following cases have been notifi.d : "Two cases of scarlet fever at Norley, two ems of diphtheria at Halton, one case of diphtheria at Frodsham, one case of scarlet fever at Stockton Heath, All the cases have been visited and the usual orJers issued. I received a letter front the Vicar of Helsby as to the advisability of closing the schools owing to an epidemic of measles. I visited the school and saw the schoolmaster and the Vicar. I found in the lower school there had had been an average at- tendance of 105 out of a passible 132. and in the upper school 108 out of a possible 145, but I think some of the children in the upper school were helping in the harvest field. The school has been closed for a month for holi- days and only opened on August 7th. I think the epi- demic is on the wane."

The report wag taken ne read. SANDY-LINE FRODSHAM.

SONS OF TEMPERANCE JUBILEE.

DDIONSTRA.TION OF THE RUNCORN DIVISION.

Fifty years ago the or ler of the Sons of Temperance was instituted in Liverpool. The early pioneers of the Society lied for their priccary object how least to fight against the system of Friendly Societies meeting in public houses. After many vicissitudes we see the Society has triumphed and not only gained for them- selves a proud position amongst friendly societies, but afso have witnessed the majority of other friendly societies quitting the public house, and holding their meetings elsewhere. What is the position of the Society to-day? It has a membership of 42,775 adults, 18,333 juveniles, with accummulated funds amounting to £190,551 4s. with 954 Subordinate Divisions and Cadet Sections in 36 grand divisions. During the past year the order recorded a gain in membership of 2,309 adults and 1217 cadets and had paid in sick claims £28,723 aid in funeral claims £4,700, whilst there was a nett gain in funds of £10,720 33 ld. The progress of the Society from year to rear has been steady and consis- tent. Its objects are to get people to be " total ab- stainers,"—that's a sine gll4 floe—and thus insures the funds against a less percentage of sickness and death,as all illnesses traceable to drink are naturally eliminated. Indeed the death-rate of the society for the past year is given as follows: Adults, 7.87 per 1000; juveniles 243 per 1000 ; whilst that of other friendly so,:ieties works out to something like 15 per 1000 per annum. Other very interesting figures are those which follow : over £3,000 was paid in accident claims ; whilst £BOO was distributed amongst deserving and distressed mem- bers.

Having thus briefly introduced the Society and its work, we have now to record that on Saturday last— the jubilee of the order was celebrated. The occasion was marked by the (Runcorn Division)—the "LifeboAt Crew Division, N 195" to give its fun an open air demonstration. Assembling at the Greenway- , road schcolroom, the members formed into procession, marshalled by their officers, and proceeding by way of Greenway-road,Waterloo Bridge, Church-street, Bridge- street. Egerton-street,and Waterloo-road,they assembled upon the piece of waste ground opposite the Railway Station, where addresses were delivered. The proces- sion was headed by the band of the Runcorn Volunteers, under headmaster Outhwaite. Then came the beautiful banner belonging to the lodge, cArried by two of , its members, with four young ladies holding ' the stay ropes. The members of the Order, wearing a specially woven ribbon—(a memento of tee Jubilee)— followed ; and the rear WAS brought up with a tableau of boat and lighthouse upon a lorry. La route the pro- cession halted at the residences of Dr. MeLennan and Dr. McDougall, and also in front of Lea's Temperance Hotel. The officers marshalling the procession were : Bros. Thomas Coske, W.P., Thomas Whyte, W. As. (patron of cadets), T. J. Role, 1.1.5•, Jamee Johnsen, KS., (captain of the lifeboat), Edward Key, P.VV.P., (secretary of cadets), William Henry Eigley (assistant conductor), John Jonas (conductor), James Moores (chaplain), A. Noden (inside sentinel), T. Dutton (out- side sentinel), and Thomas Clarke (steward). The mem- bers having gathered round an inapoverised platform Mr. Thomas Cooke, who acted as chairman, said he considered they could congratulate themselves upon the demonstration. They had that day to commemor- ate the jubilee of the Order. It tied been observed it would be a " red letter day " in the history of the Sons of Temperance ; and he hoped and thought it would be so. Tney were to hear several speeches from other gentlemen. and as they could hear himself any time he would inflict no lone speech upon them, but leave it to Mr. Leugher, of Merthyr, who was a thorough-going Welsh advocate of Temperance, to tell them more of the benefits of the " Sons of Temperance Friendly Society." (Hear, heir.) The Key. J. A. B. Malvern having congratulated the seeiety upon its demonstration, remarked they had in- deed had a red letter day led by a red coated band— and hoped the influence of that day would remain with them. Personally he had been a teetotaller for many years, having signed the pledeetwhen 16 years of age. He rejoiced with the Society of the Sons of Temperance that day because they had not only old members, but they had young ones—(hear, hear)—they were training their boys and girls in the way they should go—in the right way. (Applause.) He would warn them that the Scriptures said: "Be not drunk with wine," and again " Wine is a mocker, strong drink is raging ; and whoso- ever is deceived thereby is not Wise." Drink had been trying for years and years to make fools of men ; there had been abroad an idea that a man was weak because he was a teetotaler; but thank God that idea had been dispelled,and they now acknowledged that the teetotaler was the stronger man. (Heir, hear.) Having spoken of the erronous idea some people entertained that alcohol was stimulating, and having pointed out the falseness of such nonsense, Mr. Malvern expressed the wish that the society might grow and grow until as the result of its work the curse of drink would be no more. (Ap- plause.) The Rev W Fidoe, who alse congratulated the lodge, and expressed the pleasure afforded him by that invita- tion to speak, said generally speaking, they were all mere or less of the opinion that friendly societies had contributed very largely to the physical and intellectual_ well-being of the working-clrsses ; yet, whilst they con sidered that, they ought to say they were out of sympa- thy with those societies whose business was conducted in public houses. (Hear, hear.) Flow much temptation and yielding could be traced to the custom of but going to the public house to pay contributions. (Applause.) He thought that the pioneers of that grand order—the Sons of Temperance—had the effects of that kind of thing before them when the order was founded. (Hear, hear.) He believed that the primary object of the Society was to promote temperance in this country— which was a very laudable object indeed—the other objects of the Society, though highly worthy ones, were he took it, entirely secondary. (Hear, hoar.) Toey werejengaged in putting down the drink traffic in the country, and could they but realise the great object they would be delivered from the one curse that blackened the fair face of their country. (Applause). Fifty years ago that society had neither members nor ntaaney in the eteuetre.7—thet day he was proud to eon- gratulate tittles upon havine a great number of members and a large amount of funds; and further,the society was based upon a sound financial basis. (Hear, hear.) That success had been achieved ia the face of oppoeition, persecutioe, And mis-representation. Their pioneers were opposed by the medical profession. In those days doctors considered it was correct to give their patients intoxicating liquors ; but now, however, a change had taken place. Their pioneers had not the experience of friendly societies to fall back upon, and even those were opposed by the Christian Church. They had, however, Persevered and were now celebrating their jubilee—After 59 years of useful work successfully accomplished in the country. (Applanse.) Having contrasted the days gone by with the present advanceme.nt be wished the Sons of Temperance God speed. Mr. Evan Laugher, of Merthyr Tydfil, who was well- received, said he was there to speak to them that after- noon, not because he was a great speaker, but because the executive of the Order had seen fit to send him amongst them as art example of what teetotalism could de for the workingman. (Hear, hear.) He was a steel smelter by trade, and had worked before the furnace ; and for the last 30 years he had been an invalid ;so that perhaps he would b.? right in saying they had sent him to Runcorn in order that they might see what a teetotal invalid looked like. (Laughter and ap- plause,) He could assure them that he had not always been as they saw hint that day, but it was only another assurance that the road was open to all of their members to become members of the executive of the society. There was but one class united in one common haul of brotherhood. He had walked the streets of Merthyr needy,poonand neglected as anyone present that afternoon—perhaps more so, but thanes to the "Sons of Temperance" and other people taking an interest in him he now stool there as the representative of the Executive Council. (Applause.) He teed come there to tell them a le thins, and amongst others he would tell them why he was a teetot- lar and total abstainer and one of the member.; of the Order of the Sons of Temperance. (Hear, hear.) Still ,he was not pine to make a speech presenting those things to them in concise logical terms, but they would have to take it in the workshop language he was most conversant with. (Hear, hear.) First, he was a teetotaler because he wanted to enjoy his life ; secondly, because he was too proud; and thirdly, because he was afraid to meddle with it. He wanted to enjoy this life. He wished to have as much fun and enjoyment out of it as possible, I and considered he was more able to use his faculties to the full whilst he remained a teetotaler. Drink besotted the brain and dimmed the faculties ro he would have none of it. (Applause.) Again he was toe proud—their preachers could say what they liked and preach as much they likediabout pride—but he reiterated,he was too proud Yes too proud to meddle with strong drink. He had too much self respect to be debased by it. They might be surprised to hear him say so ; but how many charac- ters had they not in Runcorn to whom the finger of. scorn could be pointed, who had fallen from high places Ha could and did instance many such cases withiu his own experience. Then third's-Ole was a teetotaler because he was afraid to meddle with strong drink. It was deadly. He had seen it maks wrecks of noble characters ; and was therefore afraid to meddle with it. Having illustrated his points by numerous cases which he had met within his own experiencelbe speaker concluded by giving same interesting statistics relating to the society, which aro recorded above. At the conclusion of his ad- dress he was loudly applauded. On the motion of Mr. D. Bisbrown seconded by Mr. J. E. Holt (secretary) hearty vot el of thanks were ac- corded Mr. Longher, and the Revs Malvern and Fidoe for their addresses, after which the meeting dispersed.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORIIEsPONDNICT.] The police have dealt with the nuisan33 caused by the obstruction of footpaths, and especially with the loiterers about the Railway Station, in a drastic manner ; and have shown they are determined to put down the practice with a high-hand. They have the pulalic thanks for doing so.

The Runcorn Anglers' Association whom I so plainly addressed last week, wish to be heard on the subject. They say they never had any such in- tentions; and further some of their number now aver that if Sir Richard offered them the sole right to fish in the canal they would have to decline the offer cea economic grounds—as they could not keep a water bailiff to patrol the canal, which would be more trouble than it was worth. All I say is, I heard the rumour; and it must have originated somewhere ! I feel I have discharged my duty in getting out; the above explanation. To the society, however, I amply apologise, and am pleased to learn that the Intentions of the society are not as were represented.

Tha football season as far as Runcorn is concerned promises to be a good one. Tne teams turned eitt on Monday and Thursday, and side games were indulged in. Considerable merit was di.played. Almost all the old members "don the green,- and of new blood there seems to be an unlimited supply. Runcorn— which "breeds footballers, and does not " buy " them—will look in this direction for its reinforcements,and judging from the eathusiasre and talent;displayed will not look in vain.

That some dissatisfaction exists amongst certain members of the Board of Guardians is very evident. A perasal of the lively debate that took place on question of the " collection of maintenance contri- bations "will disclose the facts, I question, how- ever, whether anything will be gained by the advo- cated change? Certain it will be that there will be fewer amounts outstanding, but, I also question whether actually more would be collected by the relieving officers than by the present method. The actual difference to my thinking would be that the relieving officers would naturally keep off the books any persons from whom they considered any re- covery would be doubtful, pending inquiries. Whereas, under the present method, the collector presumably is told "so and so" and ought to be able to contribute" so much" and it often has to stand until it is found out that nothing whatever can be obtained from that quarter.

Farther, I have, some short time ago, given ex- pression to what I considered was equitable in the matter of the enforcement of these " contributions to maintenance.' The Guardians should not attempt to get all they can !—They should ever bear in mind the fact that the majority of the people from Wtloll3 they "extort"—! say it meaningly—those— (in the Guardians' aight)—small amounts, can at best ill-afford them. And if they worked on the lines of not " extorting " anything from married people earning less than 30s a week,and from single people earning less than 25s they would show far. better " Guardianship" than at present.—Mr' Roylance was right ! The committee is badly organized and requires drastic re-arrangement.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES.

THE ISSIIING OF FREE WARRANTS.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Tuesday merr ing, before Mr. Birchen (in the chair), and Alder- man Sutton and Councillor Shaw Green, a powerfully built man named Daniel Higham, employed in one of the forges, was charged with having assaulted his wife on the 14th August—The complainant applied to the magietratee on Monday morning for a warrant against her huaband, and made out such a good case against him that it was granted without demur. She now stated that she did not wieh to prase the case against him.—The Chief Constable (to the magistrates): Yea remember what I said yesterday—that she would wish to withdraw the case when she got the warrant. —Pressed by the Bench, the woman said her husband returned to the 'lll3O "rather short tempered," and "gave her knock." The injury to her eye was caused by the fall.—Alslerman Sutton : Do you remeraber what you said yesterday ? There was no reply.—Tne Chief Constable : The weman is cemmitting deliteerate perjury. This is a case where a note shmuld be taken of the evidence, with a view to further proceedings being taken.—The Chair- men observed that some men appeared to treat their wives as if they were feotballs.—Complainant : It was quite an accident. —Alderman Sutton: You didn't say so yestorday.—The Chief Consteble : This sort of thing is becoming a perfect scandal and a farce—the issuine of free warrants.—The Chaiiman (to the woman) : You deliberately swore yesterday that an aesault had been committed, and now you want, woman-like, to get out of the matter.—Complainant : He only gave me a back- banded smack —The Chief Constable : She swore her evidence was true yesterday morning. She was either committing perjury then or now.—The Bench decided to bind defendant over to keen the peace for sir months in the stlll2 of £5, and orderel him to pay the cost of the case.

Mary Shaw said she was the wife of David Shaw. and lived at 16, Trentham-street, Runcorn. She was a sister to Mrs Rose who lived at 15, Trentham-street, the mother of the defendant. They had not been friendly for ' a number of years On Thursday afternoon her ether sister, Mrs. Marshall came to have tea with her. Witness went up the street to do some shopping and was set on by Mrs. Rose. She returned and having done some sweeping put her brush down and went into the house. On coming out she found her brush had been thrown away. She was told that by Margaret Rose. rihe went to fetch the brush in and Margaret Rose followed her and threatened her what she would do for her, and then she did it and struck her in the face with her closed fist. She also used abusive language to her in the presence of a large crowd. The girl called her vile names and " hump back." Witness had been subject to great annoyance from the people ever since the summons was taken out.-13v Mr. Knowles She dii not raise her brush to strike defendant. The family jarring had been going on for some time—ever since witness had been married. She did tell the girl "she had got all she wanted when she struck her."—By Mr. Lake It was Mrs. Rose that commenced the row.—Margaret Fernival, wife of Joseph Fernival, stated she saw the assault. She did not I he wever hear defendant say to Mra. Shaw " If you call my mother bad names 1 will hit you," neither did she hear Mrs. Shaw say when the girl struck her" That is right you have given me what I wanted." Pressed by Mr. Knowles on this paint witness said if Mrs. Shaw did say so in court it was I because she was" flurried."—Mr. Knowles Oh ! she was flurried was she; but I can't say you are flurried. (Laugh- ter.) -Charlotte Wilding. 53, Bentick-street ; Elizabeth Coals,wife of Frank Ceals,49,Portiand-street. corroborated as to the assault. One of them also stated that Mrs. Shaw placed the brush to stand against the wall and told the Roses to talk to that whilst she went into the house. —Mr. Knowles characterised the whole affair as one of those family jars, of such a trifling kind, for which it would be unwise to fine his client or punish her. He admitted the offence, his client had struck her aunt the one slight tap on the cheek which had neither discoloured nor very much hurt her. Indeed whilst talking of these family jars he was reminded of some rhyme he had heard a friend of his use, which was as fellow's : "Jars of meat, and jars of jam. Jars of mustard ; jars of ham ; That these the only jars may be In a well conducted ramily." (Laußhter.) Feasibly in this case if the Bench exercised their powers under a first offence, and dismissed the case, or ordered the pay- ment of costs it would be met. -Sarah Rose, married woman, said her husband was a dock master. She had a family of ten children. She Admitted she spoke to her sister because she was " slobbering" her foot along the floor and looking hard at her. They were talking for some time before her daughter assaulted her. Her daughter heard them " agate " and said " Aunt I will not have you talking and abusing my ' mother. If youdon't stop I will smack you." Her daughter. I did so too, because her sister " rose " up the brush she had in her hand te strike her, and threatened to " sweep off her long I face."—By Mr Lake: Her daughter did not come to stand with her bands on her hips and execute a kind of a"war dance."— Margaret Rose, the complainant swore that her aunt was acnoying her mother and she went to her and said "She would slap her if she abused her mother like that." Her aunt took up the brush to strike her and she struck her on the cheek.—By Mr. Lake: She had had a bother with her aunt before.—Alexander Hammond. 24. West-street, Widnes, corroborated this statement, He saw Mrs Shaw raise the brush to strike Margaret Rose. He was no friend of either party and was only passing at the time.—By Mr. Lake: He had been asked by the Roses to appear.—After a brief con- sultation the Chairman said they had decided to bind the defendant over to keep the peace for six months, her self in £5 and one like surety, and ordered her to pay costs and advocete's fee Lila Gd.

SANKEY BRIDUES YOUTH DROWNED AT WIDNES.

THE INQUEST.

The death by drowning of James Leigh, son of Joseph Leigh, flatman, who resided at the Stoop-yard, Sankey firidgee, occurred on Thursday night feet week. De- ceased, in company with two other young men, went to the Widnee Toeatre. Leigh said at the e'oie of the per- formance that he would go home to Sankey Bridees,hut instead of doing so he went back to his boat, which was lying in the Sankey and St. Efelens Canal, near the Alkali Works. The first intimation that anything was wrong was the first finding of the deceased's cap on the dock on Friday morning. Information was given to the polica, and dragging operations were promptly com- menced. The body was brought to the surface about half-past five. Mr. Briehouse held an incret at Widnes on Monday concerning the death of Jame.) Leigh, aged seventeen, who was fund drowned in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal on Friday.—Joseph Leigh said he lived in Sloop- yard, Sankey Bridges, and the deceas-d with him. They went by the flat Effie to Liverpool on the s'h inst., and in consevence of a few sharp words they had,deceased left the fiat at the Prince's Dock and sailed to Widnes. On Thursday deceased and his mate:4 Hatton and Rother- ham went to the Alexandra Theatre at Widnes. They came out at eleven o'clock, and they and the deceased es,nt towards the boat. They left decemed at the bottom of the Soap Waste Wharf. Deceased was three-parts drunk. On Friday morning they fund his cap on the deck, and the coroner said it was very clear how it heppened. The father of the deceased said it was unusual for his son to get drunk. Rnther- ham and Hatton gave corroborative evidence. All three men had been drinking.—The Coroner said the wit- nesses were no: going to say how deceased got into the wafer. He supposed they felt ashamed of themselves. Rotherham said he was twenty years of age ; Hatton nineteen. If this was their idea of enjeyment to get blind drunk and then leave a youth of seventeen to go home by himself they ought to be ashamed. Their duty was to look after him and see he arrived safe home. There was no objection to their taking a glass of beer when they were thirsty, an I have done with it. Be did not :want them to be teetotallers. If they did not mend their ways they would be in the canal—Toe jury returned a verdict of " Found drowned."

THE MEDICAL. OFFICER'S REPORT.

The MEDICAL OFFICER (Dr. Sephtau) reported that for the month the excess of births over deaths was 8. The death rate from all causes was equal to 13 per thousand against 12. The excess of births over deaths WAS 8, and the death rate for all causes was equal to 13 per thousand against 12 the average of the last three months. The death rate fram zymotic diseases was equal co 1 per thousand azainst '8 the average rate of the last three months. The one death in the zvmoric class was due to membranous croup and occurred at Orford. Three deaths were returned from drowning. The report was adopted. TEES UNDERTAKING OF NEW WORK.

TIE ASSISTANT CLERK'S REPORT RE TIE GOVERNMENT

INQUIRY. Mr. Samuel Sutton (assistant clerk) submitted his re- port on the inquiry convened with ref :Tence to the Port Sanitary Authority of Manchester, its constitution, aid the apportionment of their expenses. The principal object, according to the draft order, the report stated. appeared to be the appointment of representatives from the authorities to constitute the joint board, and to apportion the expenses between such authorities, upon a basis to be hereafter fixed. Some of the representa- tives of other authorities baying laid their views before the Inspector he (Mr. Sutton) submitted that the War- rington Rural District Council was scarcely interested in the matter; that some years ago at the time the Ship Canal was in procels of torm ation,inquiries had been held the:remit oil which was to take away from thedistrict the townships of Glrappenhall aid Thelwall, and part of the township of Latchford, and to add them to the Runcorn Distriet ; that had these townships remained within their district they would at once have admitted their liability to a reasonable extent, but seeing that they had now but a small portion of the Canal ia their district passing through the purely agricultural townships of Rixton and Woolston,and that it was simply a water-way in which there was neither port, landing place, docks, or locks, they were entitled to ex- emption and he (Mr. S. Sattes) therefore objected to the proposals. The Clerks of the Rural Dis- trict Councils of Chester and Whitton expressed them- selves in much the same terms. It was afterwards ex- plained that Manchester, Salford, and Stretford were willing to continue the payment of establishment ex- penses as hitherto, but were not willing to bear the sole expense of isolation and treatment of infectious cases arising on board ship which might break out at any pert. After discussion it was suggested that Section IV. of the draft order should be amended soas to provide that those Councils only should be called upon to con- tribute which had landing places within their districts, and that if at any future time fresh landing places were made in any other district, such district should be liable to contribute pro rata to which suggestion he assented, as did also the Clerks of Whiston and Chester. The In- pector then adjourned the inquiry to a future date. The report was unanimously adopted.

THE ESTIMATES FOR THE NEXT HALF YRAR. The following are the estimates presented to meet the special expenses on the different parishes during the half year ending 319 t March 1900 : Surtonwood, £110; Penketh, £100; Poulton, £2O ; Rixton, £5O; Winwick, £lOO.

The estimates were passed. A DEBATABLE QUESTION.

He did not believe in smuggling these discussions. Exception was taken to this but the question as to the advisability of the press retiring was put to the vote and carrieei. The reporters therefore retir.d.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A meeting of this Board was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Mr A J Jolley presided, and there were also preset—Rev T R Matthews,Rev J H Riddette,Mrs Clay and Mrs Edelsten, Messrs S Davies, William Houghton, A Jolley, A Brooks, H Hibbert, T B Malkin,G Pillioz.H Thornton, J Clarke, J Billinge, "I' H Ashcroft, J W Knowles, William Hick- man, R Forshaw, with J C Sutton (clerk), 8 Sutton (assistant clerk), C H Ward (workhouse master) and A Wright (superintendent Padgate Schools.) THE COST OF RELIEF IN NEWTON DISTRICT. Mr. Davies called attention to the large amount of relief given in the Newton district. TrAde there, he said, was in a better conditimin a prosperous condition, in fact, and yet the sum of £65 was expended in relief. It seemed to stisk at that amount. It was somewhat singular that it got neither more nor less. Mr. ASHCROFT pointed out that in the Newton district many men had lost their situations through the adop-ion of the new Act. He knew many men who were out of work altogether. The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE thought that this constant criticism ®f the administration of relief in the Newton division was degenerating into nagging ; an he would respectfully suggest to Mr. Davies that he should attend three or four consecutive meetings of the Relief Commit- tee, and if he would do so he would go away convinced that the work of the admistration of relief was thoroughly well done, and that they were exercising a most rigid economy. The Rev. I'. K. MATTHEWS : We have not in Earles- to wn, Newton, and Haydock the facilities you have in Warrington on this point. I think we take all the care we can. Every case is gone into very thoroughly. Mr. HOUGH/ON said he had occasionally gone into the district and bad seen just as much care exercised as in any other committeo room of the Union. (Hear, hear.) Mr. 111BBERT thought the members might come over to Newton to attend the meetings of the relief commit- tees. It was only eis,,htpenee return. (Laughter.) The subject then dropped. A TENDER ACCEPTED.

The minutes of the Schools Committee stated that it had been resolved to recommend the Board to accep-, the tender of Mrs. B. Bennett at £l7 for a crop of growing oats.

The minutes were clnfirm:'d, on the motian of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. DAVIES. WEIERE WAS THE 'BUS?

IMPORTANT RECOMIVIENDATION OF THE HOUSE

COMMITTEE. WARM DISCUSSION ON CONTRACTS.

The Houss COMMITTEE recommended that Jane Green succeed the present cook on the same terms, and that the Board supprt the resolution adopted at a confer- ence of the City of London Guardians, on the 28th last, relating to the treatment of tubercular dissase amongst the indoor poor. rhe special sub-sommittee submitted their report on the question of introducing slow combustion grates. Oa August 11th it was recom- mended to the House Committee,anJ approved that the tender of Messrs. Williams and Clay for supplying and fixing 30 new slow combustion grates to rep'ace 30 exist- ing ones in various parts of the Workhouse premises for a sum not exceeding £93 1934 d be accepted. The CHAIRMAN moved the confirmation of the minutes and stated that the question of introducing slow non- combustion grates had been talked about for a long time. When the question of fitting the new hospital with electric light was under dscussion and the heating of the same, statistics of the cost of fuel used at the Workhouse were produced and a strong feeling was ex- pressed that the stoner some change was made in the grates used the better. A new grate was put in the room now used by inmates in class "A " as a sort of sample and the results had been so satisfactory that the committee unanimously came to the conclusion that it was to the interests of the ratepayers, on purely economical grounds to extend the 1.1;E: of slow corn bus- tion grates. Mr. PILLING: We were not unanimous.

The CRAiamAN : We were unanimous on that point but not on the question of giving the contract to the firm mentioned in the minutes. Continuing, the Chair- man said the coal bill for the workhouse was £3OO per annum and the Board would see that the saving of a small percentage on that amount would soon cover the cost of ths slow combustion grates. The sub-committee appointed to carry it into effect decided to invits tenders from six firms is the neighbourhood, but only four sent in estimates. Three of these were vitiated as the firms did net comply with the conditions, the only one that did so being Messrs. Williams and Clay, whose tender was rocommended for acceptance. He thought they would admit that if they could buy slow combustion grates made specially strong to meet the requirements of the workhouse and have them fixed for a little over £3 each they were not making a bad bargain. (Hoar, hear.) Mr. Hicxuate seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. KNOWLES said in the first place he would lilt to know whether the quantities and particulars were sent to the tradesmen ? The CMAIRMAN said the Master was instructed to call upon the tradesmen and ask them to visit the Work- house to see what was required. Berry, hay and straw dealer, of St. Helens, to recover £9 103 for oats sold to defendant through Charles Wright, to whom the sample had been given. It was contended by plaintiff ihat Wright had acted as his agent in the matter, and on the other hand it was contended by defendant that Wright sold the oats , for himself, that Wright was indebted to him for it , large amount, and that he gave Wright a credit note for £9 10s. Mr. H. L Riley appeared for plain- tiff, and Air. E. W. SwiL, for defendant. Evidence was given by Howard and Wright, who said that plaintiff had given Wright a sample of the oats, and told them to sell them at 2s. 61. a bushel. kis found a purchaser in Mr. Berry. For the defence it was contended that the oats were bought from Wright, and not from Howard, and as Wright owed Berry a larger sum of money, the £9 10,3 was credi- ted him.—His Honour gave Judgment for plaintiff for the amount claimed.

THE SHIP CANAL. GENERAL MEETING. HOPEFUL FEELING.

THE PROPOSED SALN OF THE DUKE'S DOCK. The twenty-eighth general meeting of the Manchester Shp Canal Company was held in the Association Hall, Peter-street, Manchester, Mr. John K. Bythell, chair- man of directors, presided over a fairtyla.rge attendance of shareholders. Among thos3 on the platform was tla, Lord Mayor (Mr. W. H. Vaudrey). The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the r3port, which was taken a 3 road, said it was very encouraging to be able to state that much bettes progri ss had been made with the Ship Canal traffic than during the corres- ponding period of last year. (Applause.) That infor- mation might not be revealed by the first glimpse of th report, but nevercheless it was a fact, as he would pro Geed to show. The Ship Canal receipts of last year in- creased to the amount of £8535 as umapar.,cl with the first half of the previous year. The receipts for the half , year just terminated showed an increase of £18,318 as compared with the corresponding half of last year. The improvement was, therefore, greater by the sum of £9783. It was true that although the receipts were larger, the sea-borne traffic was smaller. That arose from the fact that the Snip Canal received adventitious aid last year from the unfortunate South Wales coal strike. The total increase of sea-borne tonnage was I 197,000 tons last year, and in the first half the coal ex- ports furnished 98,000 tons, just one half, but as pre- vious y stated they only got 61 per ton toll on coal. Therefore the increased exports did not add largely to their revenue. This year out of a total of 136,783 tons- of sea-borne traffic only 15,000 tons were coal. the re- maining tonnage, 122,000 tons, was of a more valuable character, hence the increase in the last half year. (Applause.) They would naturally be asked if the re- ceipts were £18,318 more haw was it that the net revenue was only £12,252 larger. The answer was that the expenditure was £6,066 greater for the half year. At first sight that might seem a discouraging fact, but they would be able to show that this expenditure in- stead of being a matter of discouragement was one for distinct encouragement. He wished to draw their very special attention to that question.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXPENDITURE.

Just a year ago be drew their attention to the fact that the long-continued and assiduous efforts had re- sulted in a very large reduction of expenditure. The tidal openings had been closed, with the result that they were able to obtain a depth in the estuary section at a very much decreased expenditure. The expendi- ture on dock railways and other appliances at the docks had enabled them to perform the labour and railway work at a small profit instead of as previously—a very heavy loss. The largest increase in the expenditure during the past half-year was £1933. the rent for the elevat-.r. He had explained at a previous meeting that so long as expensive buildings had to be erected on lease instead of with the capital of the company, there would be a considerable increase in the amount paid as rentals. Fire insurance had absorbed an additional £BO4, whilst towage has cost £ll3l more. The latter item was one which would, ere long, beco-ne very small indeed, for from the beginning of next year they intended to make a slight reduction in the way of free towage. Compensation cost £9lO more. The increase in salaries, wages, and commission amounted to £1961. Of that amount £436 was owing to an absolutely neces- sary increase in the police force. The net result was, as he had already stated, an increase of over £6OOO. Oom- pared, however, with the second half of last year there was a slight decrease. The Ship Canal receipts for the year just ended amounted to £253,713. The receipts for the year ending June, 1897, w ere £198,446, so tat the re was an increase of £55,267. Let them take the expenditure for the same period. The Ship Canal ex- penditure for the year ending June last was £182,962 ; for the year ending June. 1897, was £191,847, showing a decrease of £BBB5, se that while the receipts had gone up £55,267 toe expenditure had gone down £BBB5 —(applause)—altheugh in 1899 the charge for rentals and fire insurance was £6164. It was only fair to say that in 1896 and 1897 they had unusually heavy ex- penditure for law costs an'. compensation. The Bridgewater revenue was £21,904. This was less than was hope], showiag only an increase ot £2OO. It , was, however. £l9OO more than it was for the halt-year ! ended June, 1897.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
FINANCIAL RESULTS.

Jest to summarise the ficanciAl results of the last half- year, the net revenue was £55,795, as against £42,669, being an increase of £13,126. At the end of June last 3,,,1ar the net revenue was £2,073 less than the amount required to pay the interest ou the first and second de- bentures. This half year they had been able .to pay £44.742 interest on the first and second debentures, £I,GOO interest on the new loan of £50,000 on surplus lands, and £10,053 to the C )rporation of Manchester. (Applause.) When they handed the Corporation the cheque for £16,270 out of the profits of the December half of last year, they wild they !Lilly believed that they would be able to make that payment into £50,000 at the end of the ye,a r. He told them at the last halt-yearly meeting that it was not likely they would be able to pay such a large amount as £16,000 out of the profits of the first half of this year, because that would have meant a much larger increase in tbe traffic than they hoped for. The two payments aggregated £26,323, so that oat of the profits of the current half year they only required £24,677 from the current half-year to complete the £50,000. Personally, be hoped to be able to pay mrre tban that next December, notwithstanding that they might have to pay increased rentals and more for their coal.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPITAL EXPENDITURE.

As regarded capital exprnditure, at the beginning of the year they had budgeted for an expenditure of £58,000. A supplementary estimate increased the amount to £61,000. Ot this only £26,000 was expended during the half year. The new cape nditure arranged for amounted to £24,000, making the total amount of capital expenditure budgeted for, as from July 1 last, £59,000. The new expenditure of £24,000 included the laying out of the Cornbrook Wharf for traffic. That work would absorb nearly £lO,OOO. Then they were going to lay out the wharf at Throstle Nest. Trafford Wharf had to be extended further down towards the elevator, especially for timber sheds, opposite where the wooden workshops now stood, and which would be re- moved as soon as the workshops on the other side were completed. A few more little extensions at the docks were necessary, and also some new coal sidings at Part- ington. They had alao ordered two more new loccmo- tives for dock work, and 15 pairs of ballast waggons for locks, while there were also some other sundry appli- ances which would altogether absorb £24,000. None ot that expenditure was sanctioned with ,uti very careful inquiry, and he thought it would be teen that it was , indispensable. They had only inc!uded absolutely , necessary works and it was very likely that they would , find during the course of this half-year that a supple- 1 mentary estimate to include new works would be re- quired. He wou!d now turn to capi-al resources.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROPOSED SALE OF THE DUKE'S DOCK.

They bad doubtless heard that the directors Proposed to sell the Duke's Dock to the Merssy Docks and Har- bour Board. This important step had had the most care- ful consideration, and they were convinced that it would be entirely to the interest both of the shareholders and bondholders to.traosfer the investment from Liver- pool to Manchester- The Duke's Dock was a property of great value, hut it could be sold without injuring the company's interests, and the proceeds of the sale would enable the compiny to provide inereasEd dock accom- modation at MancheAer. They were of opinion that the opportunity of securing funds for that purpose should not be neglected. It had been arranged, subj,.ct to the confirmation at a meeting of the Dozk Board, which would be held as soon as their statutory powers per- mitted, that the price should be fixed by an arbitrator who had had great experience in such matters. The company's case would be laid before the arbitrator by eminent counsel and witnesses. The arbitration wouA be as between willing seller.' and willing buyers, and the Dock Board would buy the pr 'petty, including the starlit rry and other privileges and freedom from certain dues attached thereto and subject to the cx‘sting leases and tenancies. Haying regard to the relations subsist- ing between the company and the Corporation of Man- chester the directors thought it only right that they should be consulted in a matter of such magnitude. The consent of the corporation to the proposed sale was given at the last meeting of the city cour,cil. They desired to take the shareholders fully into their con- fidence, but they would see that it was not possible to say more at present in connection with such delicate negotiations withoot running the risk of p:ejadicing the company's interests. They might rest assured that tt.e proposed change of investment from Liverpool to Man- chester had been carefully studied from every point of view, and that it would be advanta ;mous to all who were interested iu the prosperity of the undertaking. T3NNAGE. He should like to say a wc.rd on the tonnage question. It was curious to see how events had fakified the pre- dictions as to the injurirus eflot the Ship Canal would have on ,the port of Liverpool. (oar, hear.) Those who predicted it would give an enormous impetus to the district, which would re-ac!.. on Liverpool, and would reuse Liverpool from her lethargy and conserva- tism, had proved to be true prophets. It was stated ra.ently that the increase of Liverpool shipping during the year ended June last was 718,740 tons. Liverpool peopl', commenting on that fact, quite omitted to draw attentlon to the fact that only 515,770 of that increase paid dock dues and loaded and discharged their cargoes at the Liverpool docks. The remaining 202,970 tons

only passed by Liverpool, paying the Conservancydue • to ports in the upper Mersey, particularly on the Shi p Canal. They might look at what the progress had been in their new part of Manchester during the same period, and they would find it comparatively small. They would adhere to tke principle of 20.3 wt. tons, because all their star istics were based upon that, and it was teeny the fairer system. During the year ended June last tbey had 2,354,788 tone of seaborne traffic, against, in the preceding year. 1,897,191 tons, an increase of 457,597 tons. net was not a bad record for their new port. (Hear, hear.) If they included the barge traffic of 2,747,526 tons, against 2,282,485, they would fled that the figures showed clearly that there was room for both ports, and that there was no need for anything like undue comp

===== 0002601_18990818_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 18. 1899.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
PROSPECTS.

The prospects for the Current half-year were on the whole encouraging, but it was necessary to remember that they had had to compare with a particularly Rood half-year. They bad had a great deal of assistance during the past half-year with the oil brought for the tanks which had been erected on the banks of the canal below the docks at Mode Wheel. They had last half- year 43,000 tons, against 23,000 cons in the correspond- ing time of the previous year, and having regard to the fact that the companies bad been increasing their tank- age capacity, and that a good many new steamers had been launched lately, he thought they might safely assume that they would have a large increase in oil, which was one of their most valuable taffies. Man- chester was coming to the front in the petroleum trade. He saw from last year's siatistice that Manchester stood third in the list of ports, Losnon being first with 98 million gallons, Liverpool second with 34 million gallons and Manchester third with 19 million gallons. They would have very much more than 19 million gallons this year, and he would not be surprised if they were not second. They had a considerably increased importation of iron, there being an increase of 25,000 tons, of which 9,500 was pig iron and 15,500 manufactured iron. There seemed every prospect of that trade being well main- tained ; but how far the high prices now current would check the trade remained to be seen. The cotton ship- ment season would open in September—October. They would have lines of steamers to Manche-ter from all the principal American cotton ports, and,with anything like active assistance from spinners, they would have, be be lieved, a further increase. Se far, they had had 393,000 bales of cotton, against 344,000 for the whole of the previous season—an increase of 49,000 bales. They could not get full cargoes of grain from American ports, as liners running to Liverpool would take it sometimes as ballast at prices shillings below what other steamers could be chartered at. Grain had been carried to Liver- pool this year at rates as low as a penny a buehel,which was equal to a little over three shillings a ton. There- fore, as regarded the imports of grain from America they had to depend on consumers loading general cargo. Their friends the millers were bringing some grain from Canada, and they were doing well in obtaining general cargoes from the West Coast of Africa, America, the Black Sea and the Plate. One of the largel firms in Liverpool had opened a house in Manchester,and several good cargoes had come in during the last few months.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPORTS.

I They still bad to look mainly to imports. Textiles bad shown an increase of 10,000 tons during the half year, but that was due to the extendion of existing trade, and not through th, ir eocuring any new trade. As regarded China, Japan. Calcutta, and the Cape they saw no present prospect of getting the textile exports sent from their port. Although much had Leen written, and deputations had been sent to the Government, so long as shipowners combined like one man and the shippers were separated that state of things would con- tinue. The coasting and Continental and Baltic lines had still a growing trade. It was satisfactory to see the New York trade keeping up so well. Messrs Lam- port and Holt were running increased steamers to New York, and as the trade was now firmly establishod it was hoped that a larger class of steamers would be put on. They were a little disappointed with the results up to the resent time from the Manchester liners, but that was due to the fact that they could nlt get steamers from the builders' hands. Out of the seven ordered only twos were at sea, and two had just been launched. But when they were all running their traffic receipts would be materially helped. Shipowners had had an uphill fight, mai the fact could not be disguised that they had complained that they had only been made use of to cheapen tne rates to other people.

===== 0002601_18990818_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKS ON THE BANES.

Worlrs and depots on the banks of the canal were in- crPasing, companies were increasing their instalments, caul increased traffic was likely to result. Cargoes of ore were afloat from Australia, and though they did not expect much this year, they assumed that the traffic would shortly be much augmented. The Alkala Union had completed their phosphate works at Runcorn, and There was a good look-out for the Trafford Park estate, the Westinghouse Electric Company having bought 48 acres of land, and decided to erect an enormous estab- lishment upon it. Negotiations had bi.en entered into with the Trafford Park Estates Company for railways to the docks, which would be mutually beneficial. The canal had been safely navigated with large veasell, such as the Manchester Port, which was 467 feet long, and the Samoa, which had a great bnam, and this fact had silenced thcsl who had predicted that the Runcorn bend could not be comfortably turned. After thanking the staff of the company for thrYr effort and for their in- terest in the undertaking, Me. Bythell moved the adop- tion of the report anci statement of accounts. Alderman Southern seconded the adoption of the re- port. As having something to do now with shipping matters, he might say, with reference to the passage of the big vessels along the canal, the vessels of the Man- chest t r Liners were safest when they got into the canal. (Laughter.)

, The resolution adopting the report was carried unanim- ously, and the meeting closed.

Eetters to the el:dor.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in al cases send their names and addwses to the Editor. Natters intended for publioation must ba written on one

side ody of the paper. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

RUNCORN ANGLERS' ASSOCIATION. Sir,—My attention having been called to a paragraph in your issue of Friday, August 11th, in reference to the above A i Asociation, I think. t would he as well for your correspondent, before _casting any stigma on the Run- corn Anglers' Association, to make sure of his facts as the whole of the statements in his notes in referenee to it are absolutely untrue. The Association never have had the matter of approaching Sir Richard Brooke in re- ference to taking over the fishing rights of the Bridgewater Canal under consideration. So far from trying to debar anyone from enjoying the pleasures of angling the Run- corn Anglers' Association was established to promote the gentle art. Yours respectfully, E. JOHNSTON (Secretary).

THE STRIKE OF WARRINGTON FILESILITHS. Sir,—The unfortunate dispute between the filesmiths of Warrington and their employers still centinues,baring 'already lasted seven weeks. The men have proposed arbitration in order to have the strike brought to a epeedy conclusion, but the employers refuse to agree to the proposition. Now sir, the mots in offering arbitra- tion. have adopted the right and proper course, and this proves to nay mind that they are justified in asking for an increase of pay on the poorer classes of work. Tbe action of the employers in refusine arbitration is much to be regretted, and toe public of Warrington and else- where,will see that the masters are to be blamed for the continuance of this regrettable struggle,and the men un- der these circumstances may honestly claim the sympathy and support of the public. It is only fair to Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths to say, that they have offered to give any increase of pllce3, that Messrs. Stubs will give, but up to the present time Messrs. Stubs and Co., have blankly refused to give anything at all. It is a great pity that a firm so well known througheut the world as Messrs. Stubs are (for the excellent quality of the files they make, and who therefore command better pricis than any otber maker), should act so ungenerously towards their workmen by refusing arbitr btion in this unfertunate dispute. I siacaroly trust that they wiff yet accept the men's offer of arbitration for a settlement of their differences, and let work be resumed, so that Warringtoa'e oldest industry may continue to prosper, and so helo to maintain the name and reputation Warrington has, for the excellency of its many and yarieii manufactures. Yours sincerely, one who believes in ARBITRATION. Warrington, August 16th, 1899.
